{\color{olive}\large\sffamily SURREAL AND SURCOMPLEX RESEARCH}\par
\vspace{0.18in}
{\color{ink}\huge\bfseries Hahn-Valued Measures and Probability}\par
\vspace{0.14in}
{\Large Strong atomicity, coefficientwise extension,\\[3pt]
and hidden negative mass}\par
\vspace{0.14in}
{\large Merged research report from four manuscripts\quad\textperiodcentered\quad 22 September 2026}\par
\vspace{0.14in}
\begin{minipage}{0.97\textwidth}
\begin{abstract}
\small
We study measures with values in $\R((t^\Gamma))$ and $\C((t^\Gamma))$, and
through normal forms in the surreal and surcomplex numbers, under two meanings
of countable additivity. A \emph{coefficientwise} Hahn measure is a Hahn
series of ordinary finite signed or complex measures with one well-ordered
exponent support, disjoint unions being added exponent by exponent by ordinary
absolutely convergent sums. A \emph{strong} Hahn measure requires the masses
of every disjoint sequence of events to be strongly Hahn summable.

\emph{Strong class.} On every countably separated space a strong Hahn measure
is a strongly summable family of point masses; neither a common support nor
atomicity is assumed, both follow. It extends uniquely to all subsets, a
strong probability has finitely supported standard part, and an invariant one
lives on finite orbits. Coherent cylinder data on a countable product of
finite sets extend strongly exactly when their combined support is well
ordered and each exponent is active on boundedly many cylinders per level;
independent finite-state coordinates extend exactly when finitely many residue
laws are random and the individual rare-outcome probabilities are strongly
summable, their aggregates not sufficing. The strong moment problem on an
ordinary real sample set has an exact criterion. Positivity is separate: one
weight pattern with support of order type $\omega+1$ gives a normalized strong
signed measure whose cylinder masses on Cantor space are nonnegative, or whose
integrals of nonzero nonnegative polynomials on $[0,1]$ are strictly positive,
yet whose mass
at one point is $-t$; in the strong class $\omega+1$ is the least order type
allowing this. The interval example has explicit Hankel determinant valuations
and positive Gaussian quadratures at non-Archimedean nodes.

\emph{Coefficientwise class.} A transfinite null-ideal criterion decides
positivity, and two exponents already allow positive cylinders with a negative
singleton. Independent Bernoulli coordinates perturbed by infinitesimals on one
common well-ordered exponent set extend exactly under a weighted $\ell^2$
condition on every exponent row around baselines uniformly separated from $0$
and $1$, and under an $\ell^1$ condition at deterministic baselines, positively
on all Borel sets; an i.i.d.\ Bernoulli law extends if and only if its bias is
real. The threshold $\omega+1$ and the finite real shadow hold in the strong
class only (Section~\ref{meas:sub:traps}).

\emph{Countably many exponents.} With a countable exponent set a
coefficientwise measure has a Hahn-valued density with respect to one finite real control measure, and is positive exactly
when that density is nonnegative almost everywhere, the pointwise form of the
null-ideal criterion. This yields a Hahn--Jordan decomposition, an integral of
Hahn-valued functions with a full Radon--Nikodym correspondence, products and
Fubini, and conditional expectations, which may leave the original integrable
class.
\end{abstract}
\end{minipage}
\vfill
\begin{minipage}{0.97\textwidth}
\small
\textbf{Status.} A merged AI-assisted research report with written
mathematical proofs and four exact finite check suites. The
strong-class classification, the extension criteria, the threshold and the
coefficientwise product thresholds are offered as proposed original
contributions, not as certified priority; the countable-scale theory is
partly a reformulation of classical scalar measure theory. This is \emph{not} a first
non-Archimedean Kolmogorov extension theorem (Ludkovsky and Khrennikov proved
one under different axioms) and not a first non-Archimedean probability
theory. No named published conjecture is claimed solved; the proofs have not
been independently refereed. Current Lean coverage is recorded separately in
\path{docs/FORMALIZATION.md}; it includes the strong atomicity core and the
null-ideal criterion, not all results of this report.
\end{minipage}
\end{titlepage}

\section{The question, the two axioms, and the results}\label{meas:sec:intro}

\subsection{Infinite addition is the real issue}\label{meas:sub:question}

Suppose a coin has probability $\omega^{-1}$ of success. For every ordinary
positive integer $n$ its proposed independent $n$-toss law is perfectly
meaningful:
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:finite-intro}
 P_n(x_1,\ldots,x_n)=\prod_{j=1}^n p_j^{x_j}(1-p_j)^{1-x_j},
 \qquad x_j\in\{0,1\},\quad p_j=\omega^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Every weight is nonnegative, the weights sum to one, and deleting the last
coordinate recovers the preceding law. Does this consistent family define a
probability on infinite sequences?

There is no answer until one says what the probabilities of countably many
disjoint events add up to. Replacing real probabilities by nonnegative
surreal numbers does not settle this. Surreal normal forms carry one exact
addition rule: a family is summable when the union of the supports of its
members is well ordered and only finitely many members contribute to each
monomial~\cite{repofound}. The rule is exact and useful in formal algebra, but
it is far more restrictive than convergence of a real series: the ordinary
convergent series $\sum_{n\ge1}2^{-n}$ is not a strong Hahn sum when its
terms are read as constant series. A second, more permissive rule adds
coefficient by coefficient, by ordinary absolutely convergent sums of real or
complex numbers, and asks nothing of the family as a whole.

This report determines what each of the two rules allows. Under the first
rule the answer is rigid and complete: every measure is a strong sum of point
masses, and the extension, moment and product problems have exact
criteria. Under the second rule diffuse laws survive, and the product problem
has sharp sequence-space thresholds. In both, positivity of all finite tests
does not imply positivity of the measure, but for different reasons and at
different support sizes.

\subsection{The two axioms and the plan of the report}\label{meas:sub:plan}

Throughout, $\Gamma$ is a set-sized ordered abelian group; no divisibility is
assumed except in the one place, Gaussian quadrature, where it is stated. We
use $\KR=\R((t^\Gamma))$ and $\KC=\C((t^\Gamma))$, with increasing supports, so
that a positive exponent is an infinitesimal scale.

Two definitions are set side by side in Part~\ref{meas:part:axioms}
(Definitions~\ref{meas:def:coefficientwise} and~\ref{meas:def:strong}):
\begin{itemize}
\item a \emph{coefficientwise Hahn measure}
$\mu=\sum_{\gamma\in W}\mu_\gamma t^\gamma$ has one well-ordered exponent set
$W$ and ordinary finite signed or complex measures $\mu_\gamma$; for disjoint
events, $\coef{\gamma}{\mu(\bigcup_nA_n)}=\sum_n\mu_\gamma(A_n)$ is an ordinary
absolutely convergent sum;
\item a \emph{strong Hahn measure} satisfies, for every disjoint sequence of
events, that the family $(\mu(A_n))_n$ is strongly Hahn summable with strong
sum $\mu(\bigcup_nA_n)$.
\end{itemize}
The first framing, the same Kolmogorov and moment questions asked under the
two axioms, is that of source~15. On countably separated spaces the strong
Hahn measures are exactly the coefficientwise Hahn measures whose coefficient
measures are finitely atomic (Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:two-axioms}); so the
strong class is a subclass, and a very small one.

The report has two spines. Parts~\ref{meas:part:strong}--\ref{meas:part:moments}
treat the strong class: classification (sources~10 and~11, independently),
extension on product spaces (source~10), and the moment problem with its hidden
negative mass (source~11, with the Cantor-space form from source~10).
Part~\ref{meas:part:coef} treats the coefficientwise class: product
thresholds and the null-ideal criterion (source~15), then, for countably many
exponents, a real control measure, decompositions, integration of Hahn-valued
functions, products and conditioning (source~16,
Sections~\ref{meas:cw:sec:control}--\ref{meas:cw:sec:boundary}). Source~16 also
adds a third reading of countable addition, by limits in the surreal order, to
Part~\ref{meas:part:axioms} (Section~\ref{meas:cw:sub:orderlimits}).
Part~\ref{meas:part:scope} collects the scope, the non-claims of all four
sources, the open questions, the four check suites and the provenance.

\subsection{Principal results}\label{meas:sub:results}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\Alph*.},leftmargin=2em]
\item \textbf{Strong atomicity with automatic common support}
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}; sources 10 and 11). On a countably separated
measurable space, a real or complex strong Hahn measure has the form
$\mu(A)=\hsum_{x\in A}w_x$ for one strongly summable family $(w_x)$; it extends
uniquely to all subsets; it is positive exactly when every $w_x\ge0$. A map is a
strong Hahn measure exactly when each of its coefficient functions is a finite
combination of point masses (Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:coefficient-test}); no
common support is assumed anywhere. Invariant strong probabilities live on
finite orbits (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:orbits}, source 15, here on countably
separated spaces).
\item \textbf{Strong extension on product spaces} (source 10). Coherent
cylinder data on $\prod_nE_n$, $E_n$ finite, have a strong extension exactly
when the combined support is well ordered and each exponent is active on a
bounded number of cylinders per level (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder}).
Independent finite-state coordinates have a positive strong extension exactly
when finitely many coordinates are random at the residue level and the
individual rare-entry probabilities are strongly summable
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:independent}); testing aggregated rare probabilities is
not enough (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:coarse}).
\item \textbf{The strong moment problem and hidden negative mass} (source 11,
with source 10). A moment sequence in $\KR$ comes from a positive strong
probability on an ordinary real sample set exactly under three conditions:
a common well-ordered support, finite signed atomic coefficient rows, and
nonnegative reassembled point weights (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:momentcriterion}).
One weight pattern (Definition~\ref{meas:def:pattern}) gives, on Cantor space,
nonnegative cylinder masses with a negative singleton
(Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:hidden-positive}), and on $[0,1]$ a functional
strictly positive on every nonzero Hahn polynomial nonnegative on $[0,1]$
whose unique strong representing measure has mass $-t$ at $0$
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example}). In the strong class the least support order
type allowing this is $\omega+1$, for cylinder tests and for polynomial tests
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:threshold}). Hankel determinant valuations
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:determinants}) and Gaussian quadratures with
non-Archimedean nodes (Theorems~\ref{meas:thm:gauss}
and~\ref{meas:thm:quadratureresidues}) are computed exactly.
\item \textbf{The coefficientwise class} (source 15). Signed extension of
cylinder data is decided per exponent by total variation
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:extension}); positivity by the transfinite null-ideal
criterion (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal}; also proved independently by
source 18 of the same batch). Independent Bernoulli perturbations extend
exactly under a weighted $\ell^2$ row condition at interior baselines
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:interior}), an $\ell^1$ row condition at deterministic
baselines (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:boundary}), and their combination
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:mixed}); an i.i.d.\ Bernoulli law extends if and only
if its bias is real (Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:iid}).
\item \textbf{Countably many scales} (source 16). A coefficientwise measure with a
countable exponent set has a Hahn-valued density $\theta$ with respect to one finite
real control, and is positive exactly when $\theta\ge0$ almost everywhere
(Theorem~\ref{meas:cw:thm:representation}); for countable exponent sets this is
the pointwise form of the null-ideal criterion
(Proposition~\ref{meas:cw:prop:nullideal}, added in the merge), and it contains
Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:dominated} (Remark~\ref{meas:cw:rem:dominated}). There
follow a Hahn--Jordan decomposition with attained order variation
(Theorems~\ref{meas:cw:thm:jordan} and~\ref{meas:cw:thm:variation}), a
density-adapted integral of Hahn-valued functions with an exact Radon--Nikodym
correspondence even where the derivative has no common well-ordered support
(Theorem~\ref{meas:cw:thm:rn}, Example~\ref{meas:cw:ex:rn-support}), Lebesgue
decomposition, dominated convergence, positive products and Fubini
(Theorems~\ref{meas:cw:thm:product} and~\ref{meas:cw:thm:fubini}), and
conditional expectation on the restricted $\sigma$-algebra
(Theorem~\ref{meas:cw:thm:conditional}), which can leave the original integrable
class (Example~\ref{meas:cw:ex:conditional-fail}) but not in a bounded
unit-leading class (Theorem~\ref{meas:cw:thm:unit-conditional}). Additivity by
limits in the surreal order allows only finitely many nonzero masses
(Corollary~\ref{meas:cw:cor:orderlimits}).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Sources, and where the merge had to choose}\label{meas:sub:provenance}

The report is built from four manuscripts, all dated 22 September 2026. Three
(sources 10, 11 and 15) were written independently against the same repository
snapshot
\begin{center}\small\pin\end{center}
of \repo~\cite{repo}, and none of them read the other two; their batch numbers
are kept as source numbers. The fourth, source~16, arrived in a later delivery
(its manuscript~06) and continues the local numbering. It was written against
the later snapshot \pinb{} and read this report as it then stood (the version
built from sources 10, 11 and 15, 64 pages), so it is not independent of them;
it attributes the strong/coefficientwise distinction, the strong atomicity
results and the null-ideal criterion to this report. The programs and data files
in this directory carry the source numbers as prefixes.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{0.05\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
No. & Manuscript & Contribution to this report\\
\midrule

extension criteria, and hidden positivity obstructions} (base) &
Setting and scope statements; scalar lemma; atomic classification and its
corollaries; countable-separation counterexample; cylinder criterion;
Cantor-space hidden negative atom and the cylinder form of the $\omega+1$
threshold; labelled-word calculus; independent-coordinate criterion;
coarse-graining counterexample; surreal transfer and exponent enlargement;

Hahn Measures over the Surreals} &
The same classification, with the deterministic common-support lemma used
here; moment criterion; finite visibility; valuation barriers; the interval
example and its Hausdorff inequalities; the polynomial form of the
$\omega+1$ threshold; Hankel determinants and Jacobi scales; Gaussian

15 & \emph{Infinite Products of Surreal Probabilities: sharp $\ell^1/\ell^2$

The two-axiom framing; the coefficientwise extension test; the null-ideal
positivity criterion; the positive-cylinder counterexample with two
exponents; interior $\ell^2$, boundary $\ell^1$ and mixed thresholds; the

conditioning; finite-orbit rigidity and exchangeability.\\[3pt]

measures, density-adapted integrals, and the limits of classical measure

Order-limit and supremum obstructions; the countable-scale class, control
measure, sign lemma and density criterion; completion, Hahn--Jordan
decomposition, attained variation, lattice; construction and extension from an

Lebesgue decomposition; convergence theorems and counterexamples; products,
Fubini, Cauchy--Schwarz; leading-scale conditioning, the worked two-atom law,

\end{center}

Where the sources overlap the merge prints one statement and names every
source that proved it. The choices were these.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item \emph{Base.} Source~10 has the weakest hypotheses on the strong spine
(any countably separated space; no common support, divisibility, bounded
variation or compactness) and the most careful scope statement, and is the
base. Its text, labels and notation were rewritten with the prefix
\path{meas:}.
\item \emph{Framing.} Part~\ref{meas:part:axioms} adopts source~15's two
definitions side by side. Source~15 called its class
``coefficientwise-regular'' and defined it on compact metric spaces; here it
is called \emph{coefficientwise} and defined on an arbitrary measurable
space, while every theorem keeps the space hypotheses under which its source
proved it.
\item \emph{Classification.} Source~10's Theorem ``Atomicity with automatic
common support'' and source~11's ``Atomic classification and unique
extension'' are the same theorem with the same scalar lemma; it is printed
once (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}). The common support is proved by
source~11's deterministic row-selection argument
(Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:global}); source~10's randomized selection lemma is kept
as a second route (Remark~\ref{meas:rem:selection}). Source~10's sentence that
the randomized argument is needed for the coefficientwise criterion is
withdrawn, since the deterministic argument proves that criterion as well.
Source~15's strong classification (for coefficientwise measures on compact
metric spaces with a common support \emph{assumed}) is the special case
recorded in Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:two-axioms}; it is not presented as more
general.
\item \emph{Duplicates printed once.} The scalar lemma (10, 11); the
finitely supported standard part (10, 11); uncountably many infinitesimal atoms
(10, 11, 15); integration of scalar functions (10, 11); the Neumann support
lemma (10, 15); the weight pattern of the hidden negative atom (10 and 11 use
identical weights at different sample points); the $\omega+1$ threshold (10
for cylinder tests, 11 for polynomial tests: Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:threshold}
has one part for each); exponent enlargement (10, 11, 15).
\item \emph{Strong results of source 15 re-derived.} Finite-orbit rigidity and
exchangeability were proved by source~15 for coefficientwise measures on
compact metric spaces with a common support; they are re-derived here from
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} on countably separated spaces, and they subsume
source~11's transitive-invariance corollary. Source~15's finite-row criterion
at a deterministic baseline is the common-support special case of source~10's
Bernoulli criterion (Remark~\ref{meas:rem:rowfinite}).
\item \emph{Null ideal.} The positivity criterion is stated canonically as
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal}, on an arbitrary measurable space, with the
negative-part and control-measure forms in which source~18 of the same batch
proved it independently for real regular Borel measures on compact Hausdorff
spaces. Source~18 is placed as the separate report
\path{surcomplex/hahn-herglotz-positivity}; none of its other material is
imported.
\item \emph{Citations instead of proofs.} Strong summability and the
$n/(n+1)$ example are cited from the foundations report, the Neumann lemma from
its positive-support section, and the finite spectral theorem that source~11
reproved from the spectral-theory report (Section~\ref{meas:sub:collection}).
\item \emph{Placement of source 16.} Its obstructions to order limits and
suprema form Section~\ref{meas:cw:sub:orderlimits} of Part~\ref{meas:part:axioms};
the rest forms Sections~\ref{meas:cw:sec:control}--\ref{meas:cw:sec:boundary}, at
the end of Part~\ref{meas:part:coef}. Its labels carry the sub-prefix
\path{meas:cw:}. Sections~1--24 and every earlier theorem number are unchanged;
the sections of Part~\ref{meas:part:scope} moved from 25--29 to 32--36.
\item \emph{Notation of source 16.} Its density, its ``coherent'' functions, its
domain $\mathcal D(\mu)$, its order variation and its $\varepsilon$ are renamed
(Convention~\ref{meas:cw:conv:notation}), because each collides with a symbol or
word already used here.
\item \emph{Duplicates of source 16 printed once.} Convergence of set-indexed
nets in the surreal order and the absence of suprema are the foundations
report's (\path{found:thm:discrete}, \path{found:sub:notdedekind}); the support
calculus is Lemmas~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra} and~\ref{meas:lem:neumann}; the
normal-form embedding is Convention~\ref{meas:conv:surreal}; the two-scale
positivity obstruction on Cantor space is Example~\ref{meas:ex:negative}; the
classical shadow is Remark~\ref{meas:rem:probability-exponents}; conditioning on
an event is Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:conditioning}; its uncountable atomic
example is of the kind in Remark~\ref{meas:rem:many-atoms}. Each is credited to
both where it occurs.
\item \emph{Density criterion and null-ideal criterion.} Source~16 calls its
positivity theorem the countable-scale density form of
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal}. The exact relation is stated and proved as
Proposition~\ref{meas:cw:prop:nullideal}, added in the merge; the relation to
Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:dominated} is Remark~\ref{meas:cw:rem:dominated}. Both
proofs are kept (Remark~\ref{meas:cw:rem:routes}): source~16's sign lemma is a
second route to Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal} for countable exponent sets, and
Theorem~\ref{meas:cw:thm:product} is a second route to the positivity in
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:mixed} when $S$ is countable. Source~16's proofs were
reread for hidden uses of countability before any was attached to a statement
of this report; none is attached to an uncountable exponent set.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Position relative to the collection}\label{meas:sub:collection}

\paragraph{Foundations.} Strong summability is the collection's definition
\path{found:eq:summability}; the example $\sum_nt^{n/(n+1)}$, strongly
summable although its partial sums do not converge in the valuation
topology, is \path{found:ex:boundedrankone}; the Hahn--Neumann support lemma
is \path{found:sub:positivesupport}; the sign convention
$t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$ is \path{found:eq:workspaces}; and localization of a
set of surreal numbers in a set-sized Hahn workspace is
\path{found:thm:workspace}, all in \path{foundations-and-computation/foundations}~\cite{repofound}.
So are two facts that source~16 re-derived: a convergent set-indexed net in
the surreal order is eventually equal to its limit (\path{found:thm:discrete}),
and a set of surreals without a largest member has no supremum
(\path{found:sub:notdedekind}); they are cited in
Section~\ref{meas:cw:sub:orderlimits}. None of these is claimed here.

\paragraph{Physics.} The report
\path{physics/surreal-scalars-and-spacetime}~\cite{repophys} already
observes, in \path{phys:ex:summable} and in its subsection
\path{phys:sub:probability} (``Probability requires a summation theory, not
just positive scalars''), that a countable family assigning the same positive
infinitesimal to each outcome is not strongly summable, so that countably
additive non-Archimedean probability is not free; its scope table lists a
probability theory among the things that ``remain to be supplied''. That
observation is prior and is not claimed here. Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}
turns it into a structure theorem: under strong countable additivity every
single exponent is carried by finitely many points, so no exponent can be
shared by infinitely many disjoint events. At the pinned snapshot source~15 reported that indexed
repository searches for \texttt{probability} returned no matches; as a
statement about the content of the collection that was not correct, since
\path{phys:sub:probability} was present. Source~15's other statement, that
the catalogue listed no probability report, was correct at the pin. The
collection now contains this report and the Herglotz report described below.
The physics report poses no open problem that this report answers; it asks
that some summation theory be chosen, and this report works out two.

\paragraph{Spectral theory.} Source~11 reproved the finite spectral theorem
over a real closed field for its Gaussian quadrature. The collection already
has it: Theorem \path{thm:spectral} of
\path{surcomplex/spectral-theory}~\cite{repospec} over any set-sized real
closed field, and \path{spec:thm:hermitian} for Hermitian matrices over
$\C((t^\Gamma))$ at \emph{any} set-sized $\Gamma$, with a real orthogonal
diagonalizer for real symmetric input. We cite them
(Section~\ref{meas:sec:quadrature}). The same report states that ``no
infinite tensor product, measure-valued probability theory \ldots\ is
supplied'' and that it has no spectral measures; Section~\ref{meas:sec:hermitian}
shows, in the strong class, that a natural positive scalar spectral measure
need not exist for a positive-definite Hermitian moment form.

\paragraph{Dynamics.} The report
\path{surcomplex/dynamics-and-normal-forms}~\cite{repodyn} constructs a
canonical invariant density on the torus (\path{dyn:thm:qpdensity}) and
records, as its non-claim N75, that this is not a countably additive
$\No$-valued probability measure on arbitrary subsets. Source~15's finite-orbit
theorem (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:orbits}) proves that refusal is forced in the
strong class: when every orbit of a group acting by Borel automorphisms on a
countably separated space, such as the ordinary torus, is infinite, there is no
strongly countably additive invariant Hahn probability, neither on Borel sets
nor on all subsets (Remark~\ref{meas:rem:dynamics}).

\paragraph{Herglotz positivity.} Source~18 of the same batch, placed as
\path{surcomplex/hahn-herglotz-positivity}~\cite{repoherg}, works in the
coefficientwise class on the circle with Fourier moments and Toeplitz tests.
It proved the null-ideal criterion independently
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal} is the canonical statement) and gives the
Toeplitz counterpart of the two-exponent counterexample of
Example~\ref{meas:ex:negative}.

\paragraph{Finite surreal probability.} The report
\path{surreal/finite-surreal-probability}~\cite{repofsp}, placed with source~16
and written against the same later snapshot, develops finite probability over
set-sized ordered surreal fields and builds on this report's
strong/coefficientwise distinction. It answers none of the questions of
Section~\ref{meas:sec:questions}. Its example of a pointwise positive Hahn-valued
function with an uncountable support and a negative coefficientwise integral
marks the countability boundary of Lemma~\ref{meas:cw:lem:sign}
(Section~\ref{meas:cw:sec:boundary}).

\paragraph{Prony reconstruction and Markov generators.} The word ``moment''
has other senses in the collection (Convention~\ref{meas:conv:terms}). The
report \path{surcomplex/prony-reconstruction-at-surreal-scales}~\cite{repoprony}
reconstructs a finite weighted configuration with Hahn-valued nodes from its
first power moments; it involves no measure. The report
\path{surreal/markov-generators-at-every-scale}~\cite{repomarkov} studies
finite-state Hahn rate matrices and excludes path measures; the extension of
Markov path laws to infinite sequences is not treated there or here
(Question~\ref{meas:q:markov}).

\paragraph{Finite surreal probability.}
The report \path{docs/surreal/finite-surreal-probability/} develops the finite
theory. Its 	exttt{fsp:thm:rarecoin} and 	exttt{fsp:thm:faircoin} are the
cases $p=t$ and $p=	frac12+t$ of Corollary~ef{meas:cor:iid}, and
	exttt{fsp:cor:coinextension} gives regular finitely additive laws on all
subsets for the same data.

\subsection{Position relative to the literature}\label{meas:sub:literature}

Non-Archimedean probability is not one axiomatic theory, and nothing here
refutes an existing one. Benci, Horsten and Wenmackers use, among other axioms,
a homomorphism from functions on finite subsets to a non-Archimedean field
together with a generalized limit~\cite{BHW}; their continuity axiom is
different from finite contribution at every Hahn exponent in every disjoint
countable family, so the atomicity theorem does not apply to their theory
merely because its values are non-Archimedean. Brickhill and Horsten relate
non-Archimedean and lexicographic probabilities~\cite{BH}; lexicographic
probability is not claimed here. Bottazzi constructs a real-valued measure on
non-Archimedean extensions of $\R$~\cite{Bottazzi}, and Kaiser develops
semialgebraic measure and integration over non-Archimedean real closed
fields~\cite{Kaiser}; both change the sample field or the target and are
different from a Hahn-valued measure on an ordinary Borel space.

Ludkovsky and Khrennikov already prove a non-Archimedean analogue of the
Kolmogorov extension theorem. Their measures live on a covering ring, with
ultrametric boundedness and continuity along shrinking families, and their
extension theorem concerns a measure-dependent completion of a cylinder
ring~\cite[Definitions 2.1--2.4 and Theorem 2.15.2]{LK}. Neither the
coefficient-local finiteness on the full Borel $\sigma$-algebra used in the
strong class nor the per-exponent finite-variation rule of the coefficientwise
class is their axiom. Accordingly the phrase ``a first non-Archimedean
Kolmogorov theorem'' would be incorrect for this report, for the strong
extension theorems of Part~\ref{meas:part:products} and equally for the
coefficientwise extension theorems of Part~\ref{meas:part:coef}. (Source~10
made this attribution; source~15 did not cite Ludkovsky and Khrennikov, and
the attribution is extended to its theorems here.)

Source~16 adds a targeted, not exhaustive, comparison
(Section~\ref{meas:cw:sec:boundary}): elementary numerosities~\cite{BBDN},
measure on the Levi-Civita field~\cite{LeviCivita}, and again the theories of
Benci--Horsten--Wenmackers, Brickhill--Horsten, Kaiser and Bottazzi. It certifies
no priority for its combination of classical scalar measure theory, Hahn algebra
and this report's coefficientwise framework, and does not claim that the
literature lacks related formulations.

Classical inputs are used and credited: the Hahn--Neumann support
lemma~\cite{Neumann}, the scalar signed-measure theory (Radon--Nikodym,
Lebesgue decomposition, Fubini, conditional expectation, Carath\'eodory
extension) applied by source~16~\cite{Bogachev}, Conway's cut and normal-form
properties~\cite{Conway}, Gonshor's normal forms~\cite{Gonshor} and the Hahn-field
facts reviewed by Bournez and Guilmant~\cite{BG}, the Riesz--Markov
representation theorem, Kakutani's square-summability criterion for real
infinite products~\cite{Kakutani}, and the classical moment theory on an
interval~\cite{Schmudgen}.

\part{The two additivity axioms}\label{meas:part:axioms}

\section{Hahn fields, strong sums, and two kinds of countable addition}
\label{meas:sec:axioms}

\subsection{Hahn fields and the surreal interpretation}\label{meas:sub:hahn}

Throughout, $\N=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}$; nonnegative indices are written explicitly.
Let $\Gamma$ be a set-sized linearly ordered abelian group, written additively,
and let $k$ be $\R$ or $\C$. Put
\[
 K=k((t^\Gamma))
 =\Bigl\{\sum_{\gamma\in S}a_\gamma t^\gamma:
       S\subseteq\Gamma\text{ well ordered},\ a_\gamma\in k\Bigr\},
 \qquad \KR=\R((t^\Gamma)),\quad \KC=\C((t^\Gamma)).
\]
Supports are well ordered in the \emph{increasing} order. We write
$\coef{\gamma}{f}$ for the coefficient of $t^\gamma$ in $f$ and
$\supp f=\{\gamma:\coef{\gamma}{f}\ne0\}$. For $f\ne0$ put
$v(f)=\min\supp f$ and $\lc(f)=\coef{v(f)}{f}$; $v(0)=+\infty$. A nonzero real
Hahn series is positive exactly when its leading coefficient is positive. Thus
$t^\gamma>0$ always, and $t^\gamma$ is smaller than every positive real number
when $\gamma>0$. The coefficient field is embedded as the constant series.
When $k=\R$,
\[
 \mathcal O=\{f:v(f)\ge0\},\qquad
 \mathfrak m=\{f:v(f)>0\},\qquad
 \st(f)=\coef{0}{f}\quad(f\in\mathcal O);
\]
``finite'' means bounded in absolute value by an ordinary real number, and
$\mathfrak m$ is the set of infinitesimals, $0$ included. Conjugation on $\KC$
acts on coefficients. \emph{No divisibility assumption on $\Gamma$ is made
anywhere, except in the finite Gaussian quadrature theorem of
Section~\ref{meas:sec:quadrature}, which needs a real closed field and says so.}

\begin{convention}[Surreal normal forms]\label{meas:conv:surreal}
Fix an order-preserving additive embedding $\iota:\Gamma\to\No$; when $\Gamma$
is a subgroup of $\No$, $\iota$ is the inclusion. The normal-form map is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:surreal-map}
 \sum_{\gamma\in S}a_\gamma t^\gamma\longmapsto
 \sum_{\gamma\in S}a_\gamma\omega^{-\iota(\gamma)}.
\end{equation}
The minus sign is essential: increasing well-ordered Hahn exponents become
decreasing well-based surreal exponents. This is the collection's convention
$t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$ (\path{found:eq:workspaces}~\cite{repofound}).
For real coefficients \eqref{meas:eq:surreal-map} is an ordered field
embedding~\cite{Gonshor,BG}; applying it to real and imaginary parts embeds
$\KC$ in $\No[i]$. It preserves the designated strong sums, not an arbitrary
notion of topological convergence. Every theorem below is proved in a
set-sized Hahn workspace first and transported afterwards; no sample space of
all surreal numbers and no proper-class sum is used.
\end{convention}

\subsection{Strong summability}\label{meas:sub:summability}

\begin{definition}[Strong Hahn summability]\label{meas:def:summability}
A set-indexed family $(f_i)_{i\in I}$ in $K$ is \emph{strongly summable} if
\[
 \bigcup_{i\in I}\supp(f_i)\ \text{is well ordered}
 \qquad\text{and}\qquad
 \#\{i:\coef{\gamma}{f_i}\ne0\}<\infty\quad(\gamma\in\Gamma).
\]
Its strong sum $\hsum_{i\in I}f_i$ has coefficient $\sum_i\coef{\gamma}{f_i}$ at
$\gamma$, a finite sum. Zero members are permitted, in any number.
\end{definition}

This is \path{found:eq:summability}~\cite{repofound}. The \emph{union of the
supports} of the members appears in the definition, not merely the support of
the sum after cancellation.

\begin{lemma}[Regrouping, products, and positivity]\label{meas:lem:sumalgebra}
Every subfamily of a strongly summable family is strongly summable.
Regrouping the entire family along a partition preserves its sum and is
strongly summable. If $(f_i)$ and $(g_j)$ are strongly summable,
then $(f_ig_j)_{(i,j)}$ is strongly summable and
\[
 \Bigl(\hsum_i f_i\Bigr)\Bigl(\hsum_jg_j\Bigr)=\hsum_{i,j}f_ig_j;
\]
the same holds for any fixed finite number of factors. A strong sum of
nonnegative real Hahn series is nonnegative; it is positive when at least one
summand is positive, and then
\[
 v\Bigl(\hsum_i f_i\Bigr)=\min_{f_i\ne0}v(f_i).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Subfamilies are immediate. For a regrouping, a fixed coefficient occurs in
only finitely many original summands, hence in finitely many groups, and the
support stays inside the original well-ordered union.

For products: if $S,T$ are well-ordered subsets of an ordered abelian group,
$S+T$ is well ordered and each element has only finitely many representations
$s+t$ with $(s,t)\in S\times T$. Indeed, infinitely many representations would
give an increasing sequence of distinct $s$ and hence a decreasing sequence of
$t$; and a decreasing sequence in $S+T$ would, after passing to a subsequence
with nondecreasing $S$-coordinates (a sequence in a well order has an infinite
nondecreasing subsequence), give a decreasing sequence of $T$-coordinates. At
each pair of exponents the two families supply only finitely many indices.
This proves strong summability of the product family and coefficientwise
distributivity; induct on the number of factors.

For positivity, let $\alpha$ be the least exponent in the union of the supports
of the nonzero summands; it exists because that union is well ordered. Every
summand contributing at $\alpha$ has its leading term there, with positive
coefficient. There are finitely many of them and their sum is positive, and no
smaller exponent occurs. This also gives the valuation formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Positivity of coefficientwise sums]\label{meas:lem:coefpositive}
Let $(f_n)_{n\ge1}$ be nonnegative real Hahn series whose supports lie in
one well-ordered set $W$, and assume
$\sum_n\abs{\coef{\gamma}{f_n}}<\infty$ for every $\gamma\in W$.
The series
\[
 f=\sum_{\gamma\in W}\Bigl(\sum_n\coef{\gamma}{f_n}\Bigr)t^\gamma
\]
is nonnegative. If some $f_n$ is nonzero, then $f>0$ and
$v(f)=\min_{f_n\ne0}v(f_n)$. The assertion also holds for finite families.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The coefficient sums exist absolutely, and their support is a subset of $W$,
so they define a Hahn series. If all members vanish, so does $f$. Otherwise
let $\alpha$ be the least leading exponent of a nonzero member; it exists
because these exponents form a nonempty subset of $W$. Every coefficient
below $\alpha$ is zero. At $\alpha$, a member contributes either zero or
its positive leading coefficient, and at least one contributes positively.
The ordinary absolutely convergent sum at $\alpha$ is therefore positive,
which proves both assertions.
\end{proof}

This lemma permits infinitely many contributions to one coefficient.
It does not assert strong summability; for example, the constant family
$(2^{-n})_{n\ge1}$ has coefficientwise sum $1$ but is not strongly summable.
The common well order and the absolute coefficient sums are separate
hypotheses, both used below for coefficientwise product probabilities.

\begin{example}[Three distinct summation phenomena]\label{meas:ex:sums}
For $\Gamma=\Q$, the family $(t^n)_{n\ge1}$ is strongly summable. So is
$(t^{n/(n+1)})_{n\ge1}$, although its finite partial sums do not approach its
strong sum in the valuation topology: every remainder has valuation less than
$1$ (this is \path{found:ex:boundedrankone}~\cite{repofound}). The family
$(2^{-n}t)_{n\ge1}$ is not strongly summable, because infinitely many members
contribute at exponent $1$, although the real series of its coefficients
converges; for the same reason the ordinary convergent series
$\sum_{n\ge1}2^{-n}=1$ is not a strong sum of constant series. Finally
$(t^{1/n})_{n\ge1}$ fails the support condition.
\end{example}

No argument in this report replaces a strong sum by a topological limit of
partial sums, in the valuation topology or in the surreal fine topology. All
exchanges of summation in the strong class are licensed by
Definition~\ref{meas:def:summability} and Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}.

\subsection{Measurable spaces}\label{meas:sub:spaces}

\begin{definition}[Countable separation]\label{meas:def:separated}
A measurable space $(X,\Sigma)$ is \emph{countably separated} if some sequence
$D_1,D_2,\ldots\in\Sigma$ separates points: for distinct $x,y$ there is an $n$
with exactly one of $x,y$ in $D_n$.
\end{definition}

Each singleton of such a space is measurable (intersect, over $n$, the side of
$D_n$ containing the point), so every countable subset is measurable. Standard
Borel spaces, countable products of finite discrete spaces, and every subset
of $\R$ with its relative Borel $\sigma$-algebra are countably separated.
Countable separation does not mean that $X$ is countable. We use ordinary set
theory with choice; every measurable space and every exponent group in this
report is a set.

\subsection{Coefficientwise and strong Hahn measures}\label{meas:sub:axioms}

For any map $\mu:\Sigma\to K$ and $\gamma\in\Gamma$ we call
\[
 \mu_\gamma(A)=\coef{\gamma}{\mu(A)}\qquad(A\in\Sigma)
\]
the \emph{coefficient measure} (or coefficient function, before any additivity
is known) of $\mu$ at $\gamma$. The two axioms are the following.

\begin{definition}[Coefficientwise Hahn measure]\label{meas:def:coefficientwise}
A \emph{coefficientwise Hahn measure} on $(X,\Sigma)$ is a map
$\mu:\Sigma\to K$ for which there is one set-sized well-ordered set
$W\subseteq\Gamma$ such that every coefficient measure $\mu_\gamma$ is a
countably additive signed measure of finite total variation (if $k=\R$) or a
complex measure (if $k=\C$), and $\mu_\gamma=0$ for $\gamma\notin W$:
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:coef-measure}
 \mu(A)=\sum_{\gamma\in W}\mu_\gamma(A)\,t^\gamma\qquad(A\in\Sigma).
\end{equation}
Zero coefficient measures are allowed.
\end{definition}

For pairwise disjoint events $A_n$, Definition~\ref{meas:def:coefficientwise}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:coef-add}
 \coef{\gamma}{\mu\Bigl(\bigcup_n A_n\Bigr)}=\sum_n\mu_\gamma(A_n),\qquad
 \sum_n\abs{\mu_\gamma(A_n)}\le\norm{\mu_\gamma}_{\TV},
\end{equation}
ordinary absolutely convergent real or complex sums, where $\abs{\nu}$ denotes
total variation and $\norm{\nu}_\TV=\abs{\nu}(X)$. Equation~\eqref{meas:eq:coef-add}
does \emph{not} say that the family $(\mu(A_n))_n$ is strongly summable. When
such a sum over a countable family is formed exponent by exponent we write
$\csum$, and $\cprod$ for the corresponding infinite products; these are
never strong sums unless that is proved.

\begin{definition}[Strong Hahn measure]\label{meas:def:strong}
A \emph{strong Hahn measure} on $(X,\Sigma)$ is a map $\mu:\Sigma\to K$ with
$\mu(\varnothing)=0$ such that every pairwise disjoint sequence $(A_n)_{n\ge1}$
in $\Sigma$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:SH}
 (\mu(A_n))_{n\ge1}\ \text{is strongly summable, and}\quad
 \mu\Bigl(\bigcup_{n\ge1}A_n\Bigr)=\hsum_{n\ge1}\mu(A_n).
 \tag{SH}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Finite additivity follows by adjoining empty sets. Neither a common support
for the values $\mu(\Sigma)$ nor any continuity for the valuation topology is
imposed, and no ordinary countable sum at a coefficient is hidden in
\eqref{meas:eq:SH}: at each exponent only finitely many terms are nonzero.
Source~10 and source~11 use Definition~\ref{meas:def:strong} as stated. Source~15
called a measure \emph{strongly countably additive} when it is coefficientwise
\emph{and} satisfies \eqref{meas:eq:SH}. On countably separated spaces the two
definitions agree: a strong Hahn measure is automatically coefficientwise
(Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:two-axioms}). For instance, the ordinary probability
$\sum_n2^{-n}\delta_n$ on $\N$, read with constant values, is a coefficientwise
Hahn measure and not a strong one.

\begin{definition}[Positivity and probability]\label{meas:def:positive}
A real-valued ($k=\R$) coefficientwise or strong Hahn measure is
\emph{positive} if $\mu(A)\ge0$ for every $A\in\Sigma$. A positive measure of
either class with $\mu(X)=1$ is a \emph{coefficientwise Hahn probability},
respectively a \emph{strong Hahn probability}. Complex-valued measures carry
no order and no probability interpretation.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Coefficientwise probabilities have nonnegative exponents]
\label{meas:rem:probability-exponents}
A coefficientwise Hahn probability takes values in $[0,1]_{\KR}$. A
nonnegative Hahn series bounded by $1$ has no negative leading exponent, hence
no negative exponent at all; so every coefficient measure at a negative
exponent vanishes, $\mu_0$ is an ordinary probability, and $\mu_\gamma(X)=0$ for
$\gamma\ne0$. One may therefore take $W\subseteq\Gamma_{\ge0}$ with $0\in W$.
The same holds for strong Hahn probabilities.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[One meaning of ``strong'']\label{meas:rem:strong-word}
``Strong'' refers only to Definition~\ref{meas:def:summability} and to
\eqref{meas:eq:SH}. It is not any of the several unrelated notions called
strong additivity in vector-measure theory, not the formal-power-series maps
preserving strong sums that the collection's Lean ledger calls strongly
additive, and not a strong operator topology.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Terminology}\label{meas:sub:terms}

\begin{convention}[One meaning per word]\label{meas:conv:terms}
The sources and the rest of the collection use several words in more than one
sense (source~16's additional renamings are in
Convention~\ref{meas:cw:conv:notation}). In this report:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Moment} means a power moment $m_n=\int x^n\,d\mu$ of a measure,
or $L(X^n)$ of a linear functional, on an \emph{ordinary real} sample set
(Part~\ref{meas:part:moments}). Where source~15 spoke of fourth moments of a
random variable we write fourth-power expectations $\E_0Z^4$. Other senses in
the collection are different objects: Fourier moments on the circle in the
Herglotz report~\cite{repoherg}, power moments of a finite configuration with
Hahn-valued nodes in the Prony report~\cite{repoprony}, and contour, residue
and inverse moments in the analysis, finite-deformation and
infinite-dimensional spectral reports.
\item \emph{Positive.} A real Hahn series $f$ is positive, $f>0$, when
$\lc(f)>0$, and nonnegative, $f\ge0$, when $f>0$ or $f=0$. A \emph{positive
measure} is one with $\mu(A)\ge0$ for all events
(Definition~\ref{meas:def:positive}); the word never means that nonempty
events have positive mass. A linear functional $L$ on a class of real test
functions is \emph{positive} if $L(f)\ge0$ for every $f\ge0$ in the class, and
\emph{strictly positive} if $L(f)>0$ for every nonzero $f\ge0$ in the class;
``$f\ge0$'' is always tested at the ordinary sample points. A Hermitian form
is \emph{positive definite} if $\inner PP>0$ for $P\ne0$. Exponents in
$\Gamma_{>0}$ are called positive exponents.
\item \emph{Infinitesimal family.} A family of Hahn series each of whose
nonzero members has positive valuation. (Source~10 called this a
positive-order family.)
\item \emph{Strong} has the single meaning of Remark~\ref{meas:rem:strong-word}.
\emph{Coefficientwise} names the class of
Definition~\ref{meas:def:coefficientwise} and the $\csum$ operations; the
coefficient measure $\mu_\gamma$ exists in both classes. We never write a bare
``Hahn measure'' or ``Hahn probability'' for a statement that holds in only
one class.
\item \emph{Regular} has no meaning of its own here. Source~15's
``coefficientwise-regular'' is our ``coefficientwise''; its adjective was
about the coefficient measures and never meant Brickhill and Horsten's
regularity (every nonempty event has positive probability~\cite{BH}). Finite
Borel measures on compact metric spaces are automatically regular in the
Radon sense; that sense is used only inside the Riesz--Markov step of
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:extension} and when source~18's setting is described.
\item \emph{Atom.} A point atom of a measure is a point $x$ with
$\mu(\{x\})\ne0$; a coefficient atom at $\gamma$ is a point atom of $\mu_\gamma$.
An ordinary measure is \emph{atomless} if it has no point atoms.
\end{enumerate}
\end{convention}

\subsection{Two traps: what holds only in the strong class}\label{meas:sub:traps}

Two pairs of results look contradictory until the class of the measure is
named. Both apparent contradictions disappear with the qualifier, and each
becomes a false sentence without it.

\paragraph{Trap 1: the threshold $\omega+1$ versus two exponents.} In the
strong class, if the global support of a measure has order type at most
$\omega$, then nonnegative cylinder masses (on Cantor space) or nonnegative
integrals of real polynomial squares (on a real sample set) force every event
to be nonnegative, and $\omega+1$ is the least order type at which a negative
event can hide behind nonnegative finite tests
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:threshold}, sources 10 and 11). In the coefficientwise
class, on the \emph{same} binary Cantor space and with the \emph{same}
cylinder tests, the measure $P_0+t^\alpha(\delta_y-\delta_x)$ of
Example~\ref{meas:ex:negative} (source 15) has support $\{0,\alpha\}$ of order
type $2$, strictly positive cylinder masses and $\mu(\{x\})=-t^\alpha$. There is
no contradiction. The leading coefficient $P_0$ of that example is atomless,
and in the strong class every coefficient measure is finitely atomic
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}); so the example is not strong. The mechanisms
differ: a coefficientwise measure can hide negative mass on a set that is null
for all earlier coefficients (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal}), while a strong
measure has no such null sets beyond finitely many points per exponent and
can hide negative mass only behind infinitely many earlier atoms. The correct
statement is: \emph{the least support order type at which nonnegative finite
tests coexist with a negative event is $\omega+1$ in the strong class and $2$
in the coefficientwise class} (Remark~\ref{meas:rem:one-exponent}).

\paragraph{Trap 2: the finite real shadow.} The standard part of a positive
strong Hahn probability is an ordinary probability with finite support
(Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:shadow}, sources 10 and 11). The standard part of a
coefficientwise Hahn probability can be an atomless Bernoulli product
(Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:interior}) or have infinitely many atoms
(Example~\ref{meas:ex:conditioning}). The sentence ``a Hahn probability has a
finitely supported standard part'' is false without the word ``strong''. The
same trap concerns product laws: the fair-coin product is a coefficientwise
Hahn probability (with constant values), while its cylinder data have no
strong extension at all, signed or positive (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder}:
the width at exponent $0$ is $2^n$ at depth $n$); an i.i.d.\ Bernoulli law with real bias
$p$ extends coefficientwise for every $p\in[0,1]$
(Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:iid}) and strongly only for $p\in\{0,1\}$
(Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:bernoulli}).

A third, milder confusion concerns symmetry. Ordinary invariant probability
on the circle, embedded with constant values, is a coefficientwise Hahn
probability; for an irrational rotation there is no strong one
(Remark~\ref{meas:rem:shift-rotation}).

\subsection{Two further readings that fail: order limits and suprema}
\label{meas:cw:sub:orderlimits}

Source~16 examines a third reading of countable addition, besides strong and
coefficientwise sums: finite partial sums converging in the order topology of
the full surreal field. The relevant topology is already settled in the
collection. The foundations report proves, as \path{found:thm:discrete}(2),
that every convergent net indexed by a \emph{set-sized} directed set is
eventually equal to its limit; the proof takes a positive surreal below the set
of nonzero distances (\path{found:lem:bounds})~\cite{repofound}. Source~16
re-derived this with the same argument. Like the foundations statement, it
concerns set-indexed nets only and says nothing about a net indexed by a proper
class; in a universe-relative formalization the index set and its image must be
small for the universe to which the cut principle is applied. The consequence
for measures is source~16's.

\begin{corollary}[Order-limit additivity; source 16]\label{meas:cw:cor:orderlimits}
Let $\mu$ be a set function on a $\sigma$-algebra with values in $\No$, and
suppose that for every disjoint sequence $(A_n)$ the partial sums
$\sum_{n\le N}\mu(A_n)$ converge to $\mu(\bigcup_nA_n)$ in the order topology of
$\No$. Then in every disjoint sequence only finitely many $\mu(A_n)$ are
nonzero. In particular such a $\mu$ does not extend Lebesgue measure on
$[0,1]$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By \path{found:thm:discrete} the partial sums are eventually constant, so their
successive differences are eventually zero. Disjoint intervals in $[0,1]$ of
lengths $2^{-n}$ would all have nonzero mass.
\end{proof}

A separate and simpler obstruction exists in every ordered field properly
extending $\R$: the real sequence $1/n$ does not converge to $0$ in its order
topology, since it never falls below a fixed positive infinitesimal. So even
replacing $\No$ by one fixed non-Archimedean field does not turn ordinary real
countable additivity into an order-topological statement (source~16). The
family $(t^n)_{n\ge1}$ is strongly summable with sum $t/(1-t)$, and its partial
sums converge in the valuation topology of $\R((t))$; their images in $\No$ do
not converge in the full order topology. Formal summation and topological
convergence therefore stay distinct even where a familiar identity holds, as in
Example~\ref{meas:ex:sums} and in the collection's index (\path{docs/README.md},
``Specify the topology and the size of the index set'').

Suprema fail as well. A nonempty set of surreal numbers with no largest member
has no supremum in $\No$: if $b$ is an upper bound, the legal cut between the
set and $\{b\}$ produces a smaller one. The foundations report states this
after \path{found:prop:incomplete} (its subsection \path{found:sub:notdedekind})
and notes that the cut construction selects the simplest separator, not a
least upper bound~\cite{repofound}; source~16 re-derived it and drew the
consequence for measure theory. ``The integral is the supremum of simple
integrals'' is not a definition in $\No$ without additional structure, and a
Carath\'eodory outer measure is not obtained by replacing a real infimum of
covering costs with a surreal infimum. Selecting the simplest surreal above or
below a set is a different operation, whose additivity would have to be proved
anew. The countable-scale theory of
Sections~\ref{meas:cw:sec:control}--\ref{meas:cw:sec:boundary} uses neither
limits in the surreal order nor surreal suprema: sums are coefficientwise, and
its one extremum, the order variation of Theorem~\ref{meas:cw:thm:variation},
is proved to be attained.

\part{Strong Hahn measures: classification}\label{meas:part:strong}

\section{The coefficient-atomicity mechanism}\label{meas:sec:scalar}

We first isolate an elementary fact about scalar measures. A detailed proof is
needed because no positivity of coefficient measures can be assumed: a positive
Hahn series may have many negative later coefficients, so the coefficient
measures of a positive Hahn measure are in general signed.

\begin{lemma}[Infinite Boolean algebras contain disjoint sequences]
\label{meas:lem:boolean}
An infinite Boolean algebra contains a countably infinite family of pairwise
disjoint nonzero elements.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If it has infinitely many atoms, choose countably many distinct atoms.
Otherwise it has finitely many atoms. If their join is the unit, every element
is a join of a subfamily of these atoms and the algebra is finite. Thus their
complement is nonzero and has no atom below it. Split this complement into two
nonzero parts, keep one and set the other aside; the kept part still has no
atom below it. Repetition yields the desired pairwise disjoint nonzero parts.
The case with no atoms is the same argument started from the unit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Disjoint-finite scalar measures are finitely point-atomic;
sources 10 and 11]\label{meas:lem:scalar}
Let $(X,\Sigma)$ be countably separated and let $\lambda:\Sigma\to k$ be
countably additive. Suppose that for every disjoint sequence $(A_n)$ only
finitely many $\lambda(A_n)$ are nonzero. Then
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:scalar-atomic}
 \lambda(A)=\sum_{x\in F\cap A}c_x\qquad(A\in\Sigma)
\end{equation}
for a finite set $F\subseteq X$ and nonzero $c_x\in k$; the empty sum is
allowed. After zero coefficients are omitted, $F$ and the $c_x$ are unique.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Call $A\in\Sigma$ \emph{hereditarily null} (source~11 says
\emph{restriction-null}) if $\lambda(B)=0$ for every measurable $B\subseteq A$;
this is stronger than $\lambda(A)=0$, which cancellation can produce on a set
carrying nonzero signed mass. The family $\mathcal N$ of hereditarily null sets
is a $\sigma$-ideal: for $B\subseteq\bigcup_nA_n$ with each $A_n\in\mathcal N$,
the disjoint pieces $B\cap(A_n\setminus\bigcup_{j<n}A_j)$ have value zero, and
countable additivity gives $\lambda(B)=0$.

Consider the quotient Boolean algebra $\Sigma/\mathcal N$. If it were infinite,
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:boolean} would supply disjoint nonzero classes. Choose
representatives $C_n$ and replace each by $C_n\setminus\bigcup_{j<n}C_j$; the
classes stay nonzero because the removed intersections lie in $\mathcal N$,
and the representatives are now disjoint. Each contains a measurable $B_n$ with
$\lambda(B_n)\ne0$, contradicting the hypothesis. So the quotient is finite.

If the quotient is zero, $\lambda=0$. Otherwise choose disjoint representatives
$A_1,\ldots,A_r$ of its atoms whose complement is hereditarily null. For
measurable $B\subseteq A_j$, either $B$ or $A_j\setminus B$ is hereditarily
null, so $\lambda(B)$ is $0$ or $c_j:=\lambda(A_j)$; and $c_j\ne0$, for otherwise
$A_j$ would be hereditarily null.

Let $(E_n)$ be a countable separating family. For each $j,n$ choose the side
$D_{j,n}$ of $E_n$ for which $A_j\setminus D_{j,n}$ is hereditarily null. The
set $A_j\cap\bigcap_nD_{j,n}$ has hereditarily null complement in $A_j$; it is
nonempty because $c_j\ne0$, and it has at most one point because the $E_n$
separate points. Write it $\{x_j\}$. Then $\lambda(\{x_j\})=c_j$ and
$A_j\setminus\{x_j\}$ is hereditarily null, which proves
\eqref{meas:eq:scalar-atomic}. Evaluating on singletons proves uniqueness.
\end{proof}

The proof uses only a Boolean-algebra observation, countable additivity and
countable separation. It does not hide a bounded-variation assumption for a
signed or complex coefficient measure, and it deliberately treats signed and
complex measures: a proof using positivity of each coefficient would not be
valid.

\begin{remark}[Second route in the compact metric case; source 15]
\label{meas:rem:disjoint}
For a finite signed or complex Borel measure $\nu$ on a compact metric space,
source~15 proves directly that $\nu$ is supported on a finite set if and only
if there is no sequence of pairwise disjoint Borel sets $A_n$ with
$\nu(A_n)\ne0$ for every $n$. If $\nu$ is not finitely supported, the support
of $\abs\nu$ is an infinite compact set; choose distinct support points
$x_n\to x$ with $x_n\ne x$ and $d(x_{n+1},x)<d(x_n,x)/4$; the balls of radius
$d(x_n,x)/8$ about $x_n$ are pairwise disjoint and have positive total
variation, and each contains a Borel set of nonzero $\nu$-measure. This is
the special case of Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:scalar} with finite total variation
and a metric; the lemma needs neither.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Finite coefficient measures]\label{meas:prop:coefficients}
If $\mu$ is a strong Hahn measure on a countably separated space, then for
every $\gamma\in\Gamma$ the coefficient measure $\mu_\gamma$ is a finite linear
combination of Dirac measures: there are a finite set $F_\gamma\subseteq X$ and
nonzero $c_{\gamma,x}\in k$ with
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:coord-atomic}
 \mu_\gamma(A)=\sum_{x\in A\cap F_\gamma}c_{\gamma,x}\qquad(A\in\Sigma).
\end{equation}
With $w_x=\mu(\{x\})$ one has $c_{\gamma,x}=\coef{\gamma}{w_x}$ and
$F_\gamma=\{x:\coef{\gamma}{w_x}\ne0\}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a disjoint sequence, \eqref{meas:eq:SH} says that the coefficient values at
$\gamma$ are nonzero at only finitely many indices and that the coefficient of
the union is their finite sum. Thus $\mu_\gamma$ is countably additive as an
ordinary scalar measure and satisfies Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:scalar}. Evaluating
\eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic} on singletons identifies $c_{\gamma,x}$ and
$F_\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\section{Automatic common support and the atomic classification}
\label{meas:sec:atomic}

At this point each $F_\gamma$ is finite and each $w_x$ is a Hahn series. A
common well-ordered support does \emph{not} follow by taking a union of
well-ordered sets. The following deterministic argument, from source~11, is
the additional step. It uses only finite coefficient atomicity and the fact
that $\mu(A)$ is a Hahn series for every countable $A$; it never uses
\eqref{meas:eq:SH}.

\begin{lemma}[Automatic common well-order; source 11]\label{meas:lem:global}
Let $(X,\Sigma)$ be countably separated and let $\mu:\Sigma\to K$ be any map
whose coefficient functions all have the finite atomic form
\eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic}. Put $w_x=\mu(\{x\})$. Then
\[
 S=\bigcup_{x\in X}\supp w_x
\]
is well ordered, and $S=\bigcup_{A\in\Sigma}\supp\mu(A)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By \eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic}, $F_\gamma=\{x:\coef{\gamma}{w_x}\ne0\}$ and every
exponent of every $\mu(A)$ is an exponent of some $w_x$ with $x\in A$; this
proves the last equality. Suppose $S$ is not well ordered and choose (in ZFC a
linear order that is not well founded contains one) a strictly decreasing
sequence $\gamma_1>\gamma_2>\cdots$ in $S$. Each finite row $F_{\gamma_n}$ is
nonempty, and each point $x$ belongs to only finitely many rows, since
otherwise $\supp w_x$ would contain a decreasing sequence.

Pass inductively to a subsequence whose rows are pairwise disjoint: after
finitely many rows have been chosen their union is finite, each point in it
lies in only finitely many of the remaining rows, and so some later row avoids
the union. Choose one point $x_k$ in the $k$th selected row, of exponent
$\gamma_{n_k}$, and put $A=\{x_k:k\ge1\}$, a measurable set. By
\eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic} the coefficient of $\mu(A)$ at $\gamma_{n_k}$ is
exactly $c_{\gamma_{n_k},x_k}\ne0$, because no other chosen point lies in that
row. So $\supp\mu(A)$ contains a strictly decreasing sequence, contradicting
$\mu(A)\in K$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Atomic classification with automatic common support;
sources 10 and 11]\label{meas:thm:atomic}
Let $(X,\Sigma)$ be countably separated and let $\mu:\Sigma\to K$ be a strong
Hahn measure. Put $w_x=\mu(\{x\})$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the family $(w_x)_{x\in X}$ is strongly summable;
\item for every $A\in\Sigma$,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:atomic}
 \mu(A)=\hsum_{x\in A}w_x;
\end{equation}
\item the global support
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:global-support}
 S(\mu):=\bigcup_{A\in\Sigma}\supp\mu(A)=\bigcup_{x\in X}\supp w_x
\end{equation}
is well ordered, and at every exponent only finitely many $w_x$ have a nonzero
coefficient;
\item $\mu$ extends uniquely to a strong Hahn measure on $\Pset(X)$, namely by
\eqref{meas:eq:atomic}; the extension is additive for every set-indexed
disjoint family, with the sum taken strongly.
\end{enumerate}
Conversely, every strongly summable family $(w_x)_{x\in X}$ defines by
\eqref{meas:eq:atomic} a strong Hahn measure on \emph{all} subsets of $X$. For
real coefficients such a measure is positive if and only if every $w_x\ge0$.
Neither a common support nor atomicity is assumed; both are conclusions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:coefficients} gives \eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic} for
every $\gamma$, and Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:global} makes $S(\mu)$ well ordered and
proves \eqref{meas:eq:global-support}. Together, finiteness of each $F_\gamma$
and the common well order say precisely that $(w_x)$ is strongly summable, and
\eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic} proves \eqref{meas:eq:atomic} coefficient by
coefficient. This gives (i)--(iii).

Conversely, given a strongly summable family, every subfamily is summable, so
\eqref{meas:eq:atomic} is defined on every subset. For a disjoint family of
subsets (countable or set-indexed), summing the masses is a regrouping of the
weight family according to the subset containing each index; by
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra} the regrouped family is strongly summable with
the same sum. This proves \eqref{meas:eq:SH} and the arbitrary additivity in
(iv). Any other strong extension to $\Pset(X)$ has the same singleton masses,
and $(X,\Pset(X))$ is still countably separated, so the classification just
proved, applied on $\Pset(X)$, forces the same formula; this is uniqueness.
Positivity of $\mu$ gives $w_x=\mu(\{x\})\ge0$; conversely nonnegative weights
have nonnegative strong sums by Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}.
\end{proof}

The extension in (iv) is stronger than countable additivity, but it depends on
the very restrictive summability axiom. It is not an extension theorem for
ordinary Lebesgue measure (Example~\ref{meas:ex:lebesgue}).

\begin{corollary}[Coefficientwise criterion without a support hypothesis;
source 10]\label{meas:cor:coefficient-test}
For an arbitrary map $\mu:\Sigma\to K$ on a countably separated space the
following are equivalent: $\mu$ is a strong Hahn measure; each coefficient
function $A\mapsto\coef{\gamma}{\mu(A)}$ is a finite linear combination of point
masses. In the second condition no common support and no strong additivity
are assumed in advance.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Necessity is Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:coefficients}. For sufficiency,
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:global} uses only finite coefficient atomicity and the
existence of the Hahn value $\mu(A)$, so it supplies the common well order
without \eqref{meas:eq:SH}; the family $(w_x)$ is then strongly summable,
\eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic} gives \eqref{meas:eq:atomic}, and the converse
clause of Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} proves \eqref{meas:eq:SH}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Second routes to the common support; source 10]
\label{meas:rem:selection}
Source~10 obtained the common support from a randomized selection lemma:
\emph{if $D$ is countable and $L_n(A)=\sum_{x\in F_n\cap A}c_{n,x}$, $A\subseteq D$,
with $F_n$ finite and some $c_{n,x}\ne0$ for each $n$, then some $A\subseteq D$
has $L_n(A)\ne0$ for infinitely many $n$.} Choose the membership bits of $A$
independently with ordinary probability $\tfrac12$ (realized, after enumerating
$D$, by the binary digits of a uniform number in $[0,1)$). Conditioning on all
bits in $F_n$ except one with nonzero coefficient shows
$\mathbb P(L_n(A)\ne0)\ge\frac12$; hence every tail event
$\bigcup_{n\ge m}\{L_n(A)\ne0\}$ has probability at least $\frac12$, and by
continuity from above $L_n(A)\ne0$ infinitely often with probability at least
$\frac12$. Applied with $F_n=F_{\gamma_n}$ along a decreasing sequence of
exponents, this contradicts $\mu(A)\in K$ exactly as in
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:global}. Only an ordinary real product probability is used;
it must not be instantiated with the Hahn measure under construction. For a
measure already known to be strong there is a third route: along a decreasing
sequence $\gamma_n$ pick $x_n\in F_{\gamma_n}$; infinitely many repetitions of one
point would put a decreasing sequence into one $\supp w_x$, and otherwise
countably many distinct singletons form a disjoint family whose masses are
strongly summable by \eqref{meas:eq:SH}, which is impossible. Source~10 stated
that the randomized argument is needed for the coefficientwise criterion,
where this third route is unavailable; the deterministic
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:global} shows that it is not needed.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The two axioms on countably separated spaces]
\label{meas:cor:two-axioms}
For a map $\mu:\Sigma\to K$ on a countably separated space the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $\mu$ is a strong Hahn measure (Definition~\ref{meas:def:strong});
\item $\mu$ is a coefficientwise Hahn measure and every coefficient measure
$\mu_\gamma$ is supported on finitely many points;
\item $\mu$ is a coefficientwise Hahn measure satisfying \eqref{meas:eq:SH}
(source~15's ``strongly countably additive'').
\end{enumerate}
In that case one may take $W=S(\mu)$ in \eqref{meas:eq:coef-measure}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
(a)$\Rightarrow$(b): by Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} each $\mu_\gamma$ is a
finite combination of Dirac masses, hence countably additive of finite total
variation, and $S(\mu)$ is a common well-ordered support.
(b)$\Rightarrow$(a) is Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:coefficient-test}, since a finite
signed or complex measure supported on a finite set $F$ is
$\sum_{x\in F}\mu_\gamma(\{x\})\delta_x$. (c)$\Rightarrow$(a) is trivial and
(a)$\Rightarrow$(c) follows from (a)$\Rightarrow$(b).
\end{proof}

Source~15 proved (b)$\Leftrightarrow$(c) as its Theorem ``Finite-atomic
coefficient classification'', for coefficientwise measures on a compact metric
space, with the common support $W$ \emph{assumed} and the scalar step done by
Remark~\ref{meas:rem:disjoint}. That theorem is the special case of
Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:two-axioms} for such spaces; it is not more general,
and its proof, which needs the common $W$ and finite total variation, is not
used for the general statement.

\begin{corollary}[Finite real shadow of a strong probability; sources 10
and 11]\label{meas:cor:shadow}
Let $\mu$ be a strong Hahn probability on a countably separated space. Every
event mass lies in $[0,1]$, every point weight has valuation at least $0$, and
the standard part is an ordinary probability with finite point support:
\[
 \st\mu(A)=\coef{0}{\mu(A)}=\sum_{x\in A\cap F_0}c_{0,x},\qquad
 c_{0,x}>0,\qquad \sum_{x\in F_0}c_{0,x}=1.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Finite additivity and positivity give $0\le\mu(A)\le1$, so no event mass has a
negative exponent. At exponent $0$ only the finitely many points of $F_0$
contribute; each $c_{0,x}$ is the leading coefficient of the nonnegative weight
$w_x$, hence positive; and their sum is $\coef{0}{\mu(X)}=1$.
\end{proof}

Thus an ordinary diffuse real probability, read with constant Hahn values, is
not a strong Hahn probability, and neither is an ordinary probability with
infinitely many atoms: both fail finite activity at exponent $0$. This is a
theorem about the stated axiom, not a defect of ordinary measures, and it is
false for coefficientwise probabilities (Section~\ref{meas:sub:traps}, Trap 2).

\begin{corollary}[Cardinality of the atomic set; source 10]
\label{meas:cor:cardinality}
If $W_\mu=\{x:w_x\ne0\}$, then
$W_\mu=\bigcup_{\gamma\in S(\mu)}\{x:\coef{\gamma}{w_x}\ne0\}$, a union of finite
sets. Consequently $W_\mu$ is at most countable when $S(\mu)$ is, and has
cardinality at most $\max(\aleph_0,\abs{S(\mu)})$ in general.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Uncountably many infinitesimal atoms; sources 10, 11 and 15]
\label{meas:rem:many-atoms}
Atomicity is finite support \emph{per coefficient}, not finiteness or
countability of the whole law. Let $\kappa$ be any set ordinal and
$X=\{x_\ast\}\cup\{x_\xi:\xi<\kappa\}$ with distinct points. The weights
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:many-atoms}
 w_{x_\xi}=\omega^{-(\xi+1)},\qquad
 w_{x_\ast}=1-\hsum_{\xi<\kappa}\omega^{-(\xi+1)}
\end{equation}
form a strongly summable family of positive elements with sum $1$; the latter
sum is a legitimate infinitesimal normal form. They define a positive strong
probability on all subsets of $X$ whose real shadow is the single atom at
$x_\ast$ and which has $\kappa$ further positive infinitesimal atoms. For a
countably separated instance with uncountably many atoms, apply this to a well
ordering of $\R$ and restrict to the Borel sets. More generally, for any
well-ordered family of distinct positive exponents $(\gamma_\alpha)$, distinct
points $x_\alpha$ and an anchor $a\notin\{x_\alpha\}$, the weights
$w_{x_\alpha}=t^{\gamma_\alpha}$,
$w_a=1-\hsum_\alpha t^{\gamma_\alpha}$ work, even for an uncountable index set.
The classification says ``strongly atomic'', not ``countably atomic'', and it is
not a classification of all non-Archimedean measures under every possible
definition of countable addition.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Countable separation cannot be dropped; source 10]
\label{meas:ex:cocountable}
Let $X$ be uncountable with its countable--cocountable $\sigma$-algebra, and
put $\lambda(A)=0$ for countable $A$, $\lambda(A)=1$ for cocountable $A$. A
disjoint countable family has at most one cocountable member, and if none
occurs the union is countable. Thus $\lambda$ is a strong Hahn measure, even
with values in the constant series; yet $\lambda(\{x\})=0$ for every $x$ while
$\lambda(X)=1$. The space is not countably separated: the countable exceptional
sides of a countable family of measurable sets have countable union, leaving
uncountably many points with one membership pattern.
\end{example}

\section{Integration and pushforward}\label{meas:sec:integration}

\begin{proposition}[Pushforward and scalar-valued integration; sources 10
and 11]\label{meas:prop:integration}
Let $\mu$ have the representation \eqref{meas:eq:atomic}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Any measurable map $f:X\to Y$ has a strong pushforward measure
$f_*\mu(B)=\mu(f^{-1}(B))$.
\item For every function $g:X\to k$, with no boundedness or measurability
assumption needed after the extension to all subsets,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:integral}
 \int g\,d\mu:=\hsum_{x\in X}g(x)\,w_x
\end{equation}
is a well-defined Hahn series. It is $k$-linear, agrees with $\mu$ on
indicators, and $\coef{\gamma}{\int g\,d\mu}=\sum_{x\in F_\gamma}g(x)c_{\gamma,x}$.
\item If $k=\R$, $\mu$ is positive and $g\ge0$, then $\int g\,d\mu\ge0$.
\item If $X\subseteq\R$, every polynomial $P\in K[X]$ is integrable: the family
$(w_xP(x))_x$ is strongly summable, and $\int P\,d\mu$ is obtained by expanding
$P$ into finitely many coefficients times ordinary powers of $x$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Inverse images preserve disjoint unions, which proves (i) from
\eqref{meas:eq:SH}; alternatively group the weights over the fibres of $f$.
Multiplication by $g(x)\in k$ introduces no new exponent, and at each exponent
only the finitely many coefficient atoms contribute, so the family in
\eqref{meas:eq:integral} is strong; its coefficient formula gives linearity and
the indicator identity, and (iii) follows from
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}. For (iv), $P=\sum_{j\le d}b_jX^j$ with
$b_j\in K$; each family $(b_jx^jw_x)_x$ is the product of the one-member family
$(b_j)$ with the strong family $(x^jw_x)_x$, so Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}
applies, and finitely many strong families add.
\end{proof}

This integral is useful, but it is not Lebesgue integration with coefficients
promoted to surreal scalars. Its apparent integrability of every ordinary
scalar function comes from finite evaluation at each exponent. It is not a
claim that every Hahn-valued function is integrable: arbitrary coefficient
supports can destroy strong summability.

\begin{example}[Failure for Hahn-valued integrands; source 10]
\label{meas:ex:hahn-integrand}
Take $\Gamma=\Z$. On $X=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ put $w_n=t^n$ for $n\ge1$ and
$w_0=1-\hsum_{n\ge1}t^n$, a positive strong probability. The Hahn-valued
function $g(n)=t^{-n}$ for $n\ge1$, $g(0)=0$, has $g(n)w_n=1$ for every $n\ge1$,
so the family in \eqref{meas:eq:integral} is not strongly summable. Formula
\eqref{meas:eq:integral} deliberately assumes that $g$ takes values in the
coefficient field.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[An ordinary measure that is not strong; source 10]
\label{meas:ex:lebesgue}
Lebesgue probability on $[0,1]$, embedded as constant Hahn series, fails
\eqref{meas:eq:SH}: choose disjoint intervals of lengths $2^{-n}$ (with harmless
endpoint conventions); every mass contributes at exponent $0$. The ordinary
series of lengths converges, but it is not a strong family. So the
finite-shadow corollary does not exclude an ordinary real probability theory
embedded by a different addition rule; it excludes treating its usual infinite
sums as strong sums. That different rule is the coefficientwise class of
Part~\ref{meas:part:coef}.
\end{example}

A more permissive theory could allow absolutely convergent real coefficient
sums at each exponent, or use topological or generalized-limit additivity.
Such theories have different extension and positivity problems, and the
theorems of Parts~\ref{meas:part:strong}--\ref{meas:part:moments} should not
be transferred to them without checking the changed hypotheses. Conversely,
replacing \eqref{meas:eq:SH} by a weaker rule to recover diffuse probability
abandons the exact finite-contribution property that causes the rigidity. The
first of these permissive theories, with a common well-ordered support
assumed, is Part~\ref{meas:part:coef}.

\section{Invariant strong probabilities live on finite orbits}
\label{meas:sec:orbits}

Source~15 proved the results of this section for coefficientwise measures on
compact metric spaces satisfying \eqref{meas:eq:SH}, with a common support
assumed. Through Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} the same proofs work on every
countably separated space with no support hypothesis.

\begin{theorem}[Finite-orbit rigidity; source 15]\label{meas:thm:orbits}
Let a group $G$ act on a countably separated space $(X,\Sigma)$ by bijections
$g$ with $g(A)\in\Sigma$ and $g^{-1}(A)\in\Sigma$ for $A\in\Sigma$, and let $\mu$
be a $G$-invariant strong Hahn measure: $\mu(gA)=\mu(A)$. Then every point with
$w_x\ne0$ has a finite orbit, and $w$ is constant on orbits. If $\mu$ is a
strong Hahn probability, then
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:orbit-law}
 \mu(A)=\hsum_{O\in\mathcal O_f}w_O\,\frac{\abs{A\cap O}}{\abs O},
 \qquad w_O\ge0,\qquad\hsum_{O\in\mathcal O_f}w_O=1,
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal O_f$ is a set of finite orbits and $(w_O)$ is strongly summable.
Conversely every such formula defines a $G$-invariant strong Hahn probability,
on $\Sigma$ and on all subsets of $X$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Invariance applied to singletons gives $w_{gx}=w_x$. Fix $\gamma$ and $x$ with
$\coef{\gamma}{w_x}\ne0$. Every point $gx$ has the same nonzero coefficient at
$\gamma$, and by Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} only finitely many points do;
hence the orbit of $x$ is finite. Put $w_O=\sum_{x\in O}w_x=\abs O\,w_x$ for a
finite orbit $O$ on which $w\ne0$. Regrouping the strongly summable point
family by orbits (Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}) gives
\eqref{meas:eq:orbit-law}; positivity and normalization pass to $(w_O)$.
Conversely, split each $w_O$ equally among the finitely many points of $O$; at
any exponent only finitely many orbit weights contribute, each at finitely
many points, so the point family is strongly summable, and
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} gives a strong Hahn probability on all subsets,
invariant because its weights are constant on orbits.
\end{proof}

The complex and signed versions hold verbatim: an invariant strong Hahn
measure has its point atoms on finite orbits. The words probability and
nonnegative are reserved for real coefficients.

\begin{corollary}[No transitive invariant strong probability; source 11]
\label{meas:cor:transitive}
An infinite countably separated space admits no strong Hahn probability
invariant under a transitive group of measurable permutations.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The only orbit is infinite, so by Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:orbits} every $w_x$
vanishes and $\mu(X)=0$. Source~11's direct argument: transitivity makes all
singleton weights equal; if the common weight is nonzero, a countably infinite
family of distinct singletons contributes its leading coefficient infinitely
often, violating strong summability, and if it is zero,
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} makes the total mass zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exchangeability collapses; source 15]
\label{meas:cor:exchangeable}
For a finite alphabet $E$, every exchangeable strong Hahn probability on
$E^{\N}$ is a mixture of constant sequences:
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:exchangeable}
 \mu=\sum_{a\in E}q_a\,\delta_{(a,a,\ldots)},\qquad
 q_a\in\KR,\ q_a\ge0,\qquad\sum_{a\in E}q_a=1.
\end{equation}
In particular no nondegenerate i.i.d.\ Bernoulli law, even one with ordinary
real bias, is a strong Hahn probability.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Exchangeability is invariance under the group of finite coordinate
permutations. Its only finite orbits are the constant sequences: in a
nonconstant sequence some symbol occurs infinitely often and a different symbol
occurs somewhere, and swapping that occurrence with infinitely many positions
of the first symbol gives infinitely many distinct images.
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:orbits} gives \eqref{meas:eq:exchangeable}. For a binary
mixture of the two constant paths, the event $X_1\ne X_2$ has probability zero,
whereas its independent Bernoulli probability is $2p(1-p)>0$ for $0<p<1$ in an
ordered field.
\end{proof}

This does not contradict classical exchangeability theory: ordinary diffuse
