probabilities fail the finite-incidence requirement, so they do not satisfy
the strong hypothesis.

\begin{remark}[Shifts and rotations; source 15]\label{meas:rem:shift-rotation}
A strong Hahn probability on $E^{\Z}$ invariant under the two-sided shift is a
strong mixture of uniform measures on finite periodic orbits. For an
irrational rotation of the ordinary circle every orbit is infinite, so no
invariant strong Hahn probability exists. Ordinary invariant circle measure
still exists as a coefficientwise Hahn probability concentrated at exponent
$0$; it simply is not strong.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The refusal N75 of the dynamics report]\label{meas:rem:dynamics}
The report \path{surcomplex/dynamics-and-normal-forms}~\cite{repodyn} states
of its invariant density \path{dyn:thm:qpdensity} that it is ``not a
countably additive $\No$-valued probability measure on arbitrary subsets''
(\path{dyn:subsec:qpdensity}; non-claim N75 in its ledger).
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:orbits} shows that in the strong sense the refused object
cannot exist for any symmetry with only infinite orbits: if a group acts on the
ordinary torus by Borel automorphisms all of whose orbits are infinite (for
instance the powers of a translation by a vector with an irrational
coordinate, or the group $\R$ acting by the flow of a nonzero constant vector
field), there is no strongly countably additive invariant Hahn or surreal
probability on the Borel sets, and hence none on all subsets. This concerns
invariance under an ordinary group action; the invariance statement of
\path{dyn:thm:qpdensity} is the identity
$I_{\mathcal X}(\mathcal X\cdot\nabla f)=0$ for a Hahn vector field, and nothing
here strengthens or restates it. In the coefficientwise class the density
itself gives a positive object. For real data it is $1$ plus a series of
positive support whose coefficients are real-analytic, hence bounded, on the
real torus, and whose mean is $1$; integrating its coefficients against Haar
measure $m$ gives a coefficientwise Hahn probability with $\mu_0=m$ and
$\mu_\gamma\ll m$ for $\gamma>0$, positive on every Borel set by
Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:dominated}.
\end{remark}

\section{Surreal and surcomplex values}\label{meas:sec:surreal}

Let $X$ and $\Sigma$ be sets and let $\mu:\Sigma\to\No$ or $\No[i]$ be a
function on that set domain. Work in a class theory with replacement, or in a
sufficiently large universe for the set-sized data. The range is a set, each
of its normal forms has set-sized support, and so the union of the supports of
all values is a set, whether or not it is well based. The additive subgroup of
$\No$ generated by the negatives of all these exponents is a set-sized ordered
abelian group $\Gamma$, and every value of $\mu$ is represented in $\KR$, or in
$\KC$ after combining the two coordinate supports. This is the localization of
\path{found:thm:workspace}~\cite{repofound}, here without passing to a
divisible hull. The strong-additivity axiom is unchanged by the
identification, so Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} applies and proves a common
well-ordered support as a theorem, rather than assuming it during
localization.

\begin{corollary}[Actual surreal atomicity; source 10]\label{meas:cor:actual}
On a countably separated set-sized measurable space, every $\No$-valued or
$\No[i]$-valued measure that is countably additive by canonical strong
normal-form sums is a strong sum of its singleton masses. Every normal-form
coefficient is carried by finitely many points, and all event masses have a
common admissible support. A positive normalized surreal measure of this kind
has a finitely supported ordinary real standard part.
\end{corollary}

No topology on the proper class $\No$ is required. The sample space is a set,
and nothing is asserted about a measure on the full power class of $\No$.
Exponent enlargement cannot repair the extension obstructions of
Part~\ref{meas:part:products}; see Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:enlarge}.

\part{Strong Hahn measures: extension on product spaces}\label{meas:part:products}

This part is source~10's. Every measure in it is strong; the coefficientwise
counterparts are in Part~\ref{meas:part:coef}.

\section{An exact extension theorem for cylinder data}\label{meas:sec:cylinder}

Let $E_n$ be nonempty finite sets and
\[
 \Omega=\prod_{n\ge1}E_n,\qquad T_n=\prod_{j=1}^{n}E_j,\qquad T_0=\{\varnothing\}.
\]
For a word $s\in T_n$, $[s]$ denotes its cylinder. The product Borel
$\sigma$-algebra $\B(\Omega)$ is countably separated and generated by the
cylinders. Suppose $m_s\in K$ are \emph{coherent}:
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:coherence}
 m_s=\sum_{a\in E_{n+1}}m_{sa}\qquad(s\in T_n).
\end{equation}
This gives a finitely additive law on the cylinder algebra; it does not yet
define masses of arbitrary Borel events. For each exponent define the
\emph{active width} at depth $n$ and the combined support by
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:width}
 r_\gamma(n)=\#\{s\in T_n:\coef{\gamma}{m_s}\ne0\},\qquad
 S_T=\bigcup_{n\ge0}\bigcup_{s\in T_n}\supp(m_s).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Bounded-width coherent scalar trees]\label{meas:lem:tree}
Let $c_s\in k$ satisfy \eqref{meas:eq:coherence}. If the number of nonzero $c_s$
at each level is bounded independently of the level, then there are finitely
many distinct paths $x_1,\ldots,x_r\in\Omega$ and nonzero constants
$d_1,\ldots,d_r$ with
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:tree-atoms}
 c_s=\sum_{j:\,x_j\in[s]}d_j\qquad\text{for every word }s.
\end{equation}
This representation is unique.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Each nonzero parent has at least one nonzero child, and children of different
parents are distinct, so the widths form a nondecreasing sequence of integers.
A bounded such sequence is eventually constant, say from level $N$ on. At
every later level each nonzero parent has exactly one nonzero child and every
zero parent has only zero children; an extra nonzero child, or nonzero
children under a zero parent, would increase the width. The nonzero nodes at
level $N$ therefore continue along finitely many unique paths, and coherence
makes the coefficient constant along each path. These paths and coefficients
represent all levels $n\ge N$, and summing descendants gives the earlier
levels. For uniqueness, a sufficiently deep level separates any finite
collection of distinct paths, and its cylinder values recover the
coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact strong cylinder-extension criterion; source 10]
\label{meas:thm:cylinder}
The coherent family $(m_s)$ has a strong Hahn measure extension to
$\B(\Omega)$ if and only if
\begin{align}
 &S_T\ \text{is well ordered},\label{meas:eq:C1}\tag{C1}\\
 &\sup_{n\ge0}r_\gamma(n)<\infty\quad\text{for every }\gamma\in\Gamma.
 \label{meas:eq:C2}\tag{C2}
\end{align}
When it exists the extension is unique, its global support is $S_T$, and it
extends uniquely to all subsets of $\Omega$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $\mu$ is an extension. By Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} its global
support is well ordered and each coefficient measure has finitely many point
atoms. At a fixed level each nonzero coefficient cylinder contains at least
one of these atoms, and the cylinders of one level are disjoint, so their
number is bounded by the number of coefficient atoms. This proves (C1) and
(C2). A nonzero finite atomic coefficient measure is nonzero on some cylinder
(separate its finitely many atoms), so the global support is exactly $S_T$.

Conversely, for each $\gamma\in S_T$ apply Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:tree} to
$c_s=\coef{\gamma}{m_s}$ and obtain a finite atomic scalar measure $\mu_\gamma$ on
$\Omega$. For any $A\subseteq\Omega$ put
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:construct-extension}
 \mu(A)=\sum_{\gamma\in S_T}\mu_\gamma(A)\,t^\gamma.
\end{equation}
Its support lies in the well-ordered $S_T$, so it is a Hahn series. For a
disjoint sequence of subsets, at a fixed $\gamma$ only finitely many members
meet the finite atomic support of $\mu_\gamma$; the family of masses is strong,
and finite coefficient additivity gives \eqref{meas:eq:SH}. Formula
\eqref{meas:eq:tree-atoms} gives the prescribed cylinder values.

Any strong extension has finite atomic coefficient measures by
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}, and each is determined by its cylinder values
by the last argument of Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:tree}; this also excludes exponents
outside $S_T$. The all-subsets assertion is
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}(iv).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the criterion does and does not bound]
The width bound in (C2) may depend on $\gamma$; no uniform bound on the number
of all atoms is required, and the stabilization level in
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:tree} may also depend on $\gamma$. These two
nonuniformities are essential: they permit infinitely many infinitesimal
scales, and they are responsible for the positivity obstruction of
Section~\ref{meas:sec:pattern}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Exact positivity test; source 10]\label{meas:cor:positive-test}
Assume (C1)--(C2), $k=\R$ and $m_\varnothing=1$. Reconstruct the coefficient
measures as above and put
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:reconstructed-atoms}
 w_x=\sum_{\gamma\in S_T}\mu_\gamma(\{x\})\,t^\gamma.
\end{equation}
There is a positive strong probability extension if and only if $w_x\ge0$ for
every $x\in\Omega$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
This is the positivity clause of Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} together with
the uniqueness in Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder}.
\end{proof}

Nonnegativity of all cylinder masses is \emph{not} a substitute for the test:
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:hidden-positive} gives nonnegative cylinders whose
unique strong extension has a negative point weight.

\section{Labelled support calculus for infinite products}\label{meas:sec:neumann}

The independent-product theorem must control both the support and the number
of labelled contributions at a fixed exponent. We state the classical support
result in the form needed.

\begin{lemma}[Neumann support lemma, classical; stated by sources 10 and 15]
\label{meas:lem:neumann}
Let $S\subseteq\Gamma_{>0}$ be well ordered. The additive monoid
\[
 S^*=\{s_1+\cdots+s_m:m\ge0,\ s_j\in S\}
\]
is well ordered, and for each $\rho\in\Gamma$ only finitely many ordered finite
words $(s_1,\ldots,s_m)$ in $S$ have sum $\rho$. In particular only finitely
many pairs $(\alpha,\beta)\in S^*\times S^*$ satisfy $\alpha+\beta=\rho$.
\end{lemma}

This is the positive-support form of Neumann's lemma~\cite{Neumann}, and it is
the collection's \path{found:sub:positivesupport}~\cite{repofound}; we import
it. It holds at arbitrary rank: word lengths are not bounded by an Archimedean
comparison with a least positive scale. Source~15 recalls one standard proof:
finite words over a well-ordered alphabet are well-quasi-ordered by increasing
subsequence embedding, and a proper embedding strictly increases the sum since
all letters are positive; so there is neither an infinite strictly decreasing
sequence of sums nor infinitely many distinct words of one sum. The pair
assertion follows by concatenating representations of the two summands.

\begin{remark}[A fixed exponent, not a cofinal decay; source 15]
\label{meas:rem:cofinal}
The lemma concerns contributions to a \emph{fixed} exponent, not the number of
exponents below a bound. If $0<\alpha$ is infinitesimal relative to $\beta$ in
$\Gamma$, all $n\alpha$ lie below $\beta$. No argument here infers that the
powers of $t^\alpha$ are cofinal in the valuation topology.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Labelled words and subproducts]\label{meas:lem:labeled}
Let $(a_i)_{i\in I}$ be a strongly summable infinitesimal family. Then the
family of labelled products
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:word-family}
 \bigl(a_{i_1}\cdots a_{i_m}\bigr)_{m\ge0,\ (i_1,\ldots,i_m)\in I^m}
\end{equation}
is strongly summable, where zero products may be discarded and the empty word
occurs once. Any subfamily of these labelled products is therefore strongly
summable, including the products indexed by finite subsets of $I$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $S$ be the union of the (positive) supports of the $a_i$. The support of
every product lies in $S^*$. At a fixed exponent $\rho$, Neumann's lemma gives
finitely many exponent words $(s_1,\ldots,s_m)$ summing to $\rho$; for each
letter $s_j$ only finitely many labels $i_j$ contribute a nonzero coefficient,
by strong summability of $(a_i)$. So only finitely many labelled coefficient
words occur at $\rho$, and in particular only finitely many nonzero products
contribute there.
\end{proof}

Infinitely many zero labels cause no problem in \eqref{meas:eq:word-family}:
every nonempty product containing one is the zero series, which contributes at
no exponent, and the single empty product is $1$.

\begin{proposition}[Infinite products of infinitesimal perturbations]
\label{meas:prop:products}
For a strongly summable infinitesimal family $(q_n)_{n\ge1}$, define
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:U}
 U=\hprod_{n\ge1}(1-q_n)
   :=\hsum_{F\in\Fin(\N)}(-1)^{\abs F}\prod_{n\in F}q_n .
\end{equation}
Then $U\in1+\mathfrak m$ is a unit. The families
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:ratios}
 \Bigl(\frac{q_n}{1-q_n}\Bigr)_n\quad\text{and more generally}\quad
 \Bigl(\frac{a_{n,j}}{1-q_n}\Bigr)_{n,j}
\end{equation}
are strongly summable whenever the infinitesimal family $(a_{n,j})_{n,j}$ is
strongly summable and $q_n=\sum_ja_{n,j}$ with finitely many $j$ at each $n$.
All finite-factor product identities used with \eqref{meas:eq:U} remain valid
coefficientwise.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{meas:eq:U} is a signed subfamily of the labelled words of
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:labeled}; its nonconstant support is positive, so
$U\in1+\mathfrak m$. The geometric series $(1-q_n)^{-1}=\hsum_{m\ge0}q_n^m$ is
valid by the same lemma. Expanding $a_{n,j}\sum_mq_n^m$ gives labelled
infinitesimal words whose first letter carries the distinguished index
$(n,j)$; at a fixed exponent there are finitely many such words and labels,
which proves \eqref{meas:eq:ratios}. To justify a finite-factor product
identity at an exponent $\rho$, collect
all labels in the finitely many coefficient words that can contribute to
$\rho$. Only finitely many factors are then relevant. Inverse factors are
expanded by their geometric series, so they obey the same word bound.
Apply the ordinary finite product identity to these factors; all omitted
terms have zero coefficient at $\rho$. Doing this separately for every
$\rho$ proves the Hahn identity, without a common finite cutoff or a
topological limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Recovering rare entries from first-error masses]
\label{meas:lem:recover}
Let $p_{n,j}\in\mathfrak m$ be real Hahn series (zero allowed), with finitely
many indices $j$ at each $n$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:first-error}
 q_n=\sum_jp_{n,j},\qquad u_n=1-q_n,\qquad h_{n,j}=p_{n,j}\prod_{k<n}u_k.
\end{equation}
Then $(p_{n,j})_{n,j}$ is strongly summable if and only if $(h_{n,j})_{n,j}$ is.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $(p_{n,j})$ is strong, expanding the finite products in
\eqref{meas:eq:first-error} gives labelled words whose first letter has the
distinguished label $(n,j)$, and Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:labeled} makes $(h_{n,j})$
strong. Conversely, finite telescoping gives
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:survival}
 \prod_{k<n}u_k=1-H_{<n},\qquad H_{<n}=\sum_{k<n}\sum_jh_{k,j}.
\end{equation}
Each nonzero $h_{n,j}$ has positive valuation, so
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:recover}
 p_{n,j}=h_{n,j}(1-H_{<n})^{-1}=\hsum_{m\ge0}h_{n,j}H_{<n}^{\,m}.
\end{equation}
Expand $H_{<n}^{\,m}$ into labelled words: the first label $(n,j)$ is again
distinguished and all later coordinate labels are smaller than $n$. At each
exponent Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:labeled} leaves finitely many full words, hence
finitely many distinguished indices, so \eqref{meas:eq:recover} is strongly
summable simultaneously for all $(n,j)$. No uniform bound on $n$ or $m$ is
imposed.
\end{proof}

\section{Independent coordinates: a sharp strong extension criterion}
\label{meas:sec:independent}

Let $p_{n,a}\in\KR$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:prob-rows}
 p_{n,a}\ge0,\qquad\sum_{a\in E_n}p_{n,a}=1,
\end{equation}
so every entry is finite. The independent cylinder law is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:independent-cylinder}
 m_s=\prod_{n=1}^{\abs s}p_{n,s_n}.
\end{equation}
Write $\overline p_{n,a}=\st(p_{n,a})$, an ordinary finite-state probability
vector. Call coordinate $n$ \emph{residually nondeterministic} if at least two
entries of this vector are positive, and let $J$ be the set of such
coordinates. For $n\notin J$ there is a unique $b_n\in E_n$ with
$\overline p_{n,b_n}=1$; every other entry is zero or a positive infinitesimal,
and these are the \emph{rare entries}.

\begin{theorem}[Exact independent finite-state criterion; source 10]
\label{meas:thm:independent}
The independent cylinder law \eqref{meas:eq:independent-cylinder} has a
positive strong Hahn probability extension if and only if
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $J$ is finite, and
\item the family of individual rare entries
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:rare-family}
 \bigl(p_{n,a}\bigr)_{n\notin J,\ a\ne b_n}
\end{equation}
is strongly summable.
\end{enumerate}
The extension is then unique, and it is supported on the paths with only
finitely many deviations from the $b_n$ outside $J$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Necessity: finite residue branching}
\begin{proof}[Proof of necessity in Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:independent}]
Let $\mu$ be a positive strong extension. By Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:shadow},
$\st\circ\mu$ is a real probability with finitely many point atoms, say at most
$M$. If there were $r$ residually nondeterministic coordinates, choose two
positive-residue states at each. The corresponding $2^r$ disjoint
finite-coordinate cylinder events have positive constant coefficients by
\eqref{meas:eq:independent-cylinder}, so each contains a distinct residue atom
and $2^r\le M$. Hence $J$ is finite.

Project away the coordinates in $J$ and relabel the others in increasing
order; pushforward preserves \eqref{meas:eq:SH}, so we may assume $J$ empty.
Put $q_n=\sum_{a\ne b_n}p_{n,a}$ and $u_n=p_{n,b_n}=1-q_n$; each $q_n$ is zero or
a positive infinitesimal. For $a\ne b_n$ let $B_{n,a}$ be the event that
coordinates $1,\ldots,n-1$ take their base states and coordinate $n$ equals
$a$. These events are pairwise disjoint as $(n,a)$ varies, with prescribed
masses $\mu(B_{n,a})=p_{n,a}\prod_{k<n}u_k=h_{n,a}$. The family is countable
because the alphabets are finite, so \eqref{meas:eq:SH} makes $(h_{n,a})$
strongly summable, and Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:recover} makes
$(p_{n,a})_{a\ne b_n}$ strongly summable.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sufficiency: the finite-deviation formula}
For the construction, reorder the complement of the finite set $J$ as
$1,2,\ldots$ temporarily; the notation keeps the original indices. Define
\[
 q_n=\sum_{a\ne b_n}p_{n,a},\qquad u_n=1-q_n,\qquad
 r_{n,a}=\frac{p_{n,a}}{u_n}\quad(n\notin J,\ a\ne b_n),\qquad
 U=\hprod_{n\notin J}u_n.
\]
The $q_n$ form a strong family by grouping \eqref{meas:eq:rare-family}, and
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:products} applies. Let $\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}}$ be the
set of paths $x$ for which $F(x)=\{n\notin J:x_n\ne b_n\}$ is finite, and put
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:product-weights}
 W_x=\Bigl(\prod_{j\in J}p_{j,x_j}\Bigr)\,U\prod_{n\in F(x)}r_{n,x_n}
 \quad(x\in\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}}),\qquad
 W_x=0\quad(x\notin\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}}).
\end{equation}
The product over $J$ is ordinary and finite: residue randomness is kept
exactly at the finitely many exceptional coordinates, not replaced by a chosen
state.

\begin{proof}[Proof of sufficiency and uniqueness in
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:independent}]
The family $(r_{n,a})$ is strong and infinitesimal by
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:products}. Its products indexed by finite coordinate
subsets, with one rare-state label per chosen coordinate, are a subfamily of
the labelled words, hence strongly summable. Multiplication by the fixed
series $U$ preserves strong summability (the sum of its support with the
common support is well ordered with finitely many contributions per exponent),
and so does the finite union over the choices at $J$ with their fixed finite
products. Thus $(W_x)$ is strong. Each $u_n$ has leading coefficient $1$, so
$U$ has leading coefficient $1$ and is positive; every other factor in
\eqref{meas:eq:product-weights} is nonnegative, so every $W_x\ge0$.

For $n\notin J$,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:one-plus-r}
 1+\sum_{a\ne b_n}r_{n,a}=1+\frac{q_n}{u_n}=u_n^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Summing the finite-deviation products and applying the justified
coefficientwise finite-factor identities gives
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:normalization-product}
 \hsum_{\substack{F\subseteq\N\setminus J\text{ finite}\\a_n\ne b_n\ (n\in F)}}
 \prod_{n\in F}r_{n,a_n}
 =\hprod_{n\notin J}\Bigl(1+\sum_{a\ne b_n}r_{n,a}\Bigr)=U^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The finite sum over the coordinates in $J$ of $\prod_{j\in J}p_{j,x_j}$ is $1$,
so $\hsum_xW_x=1$ by
\eqref{meas:eq:product-weights}--\eqref{meas:eq:normalization-product}. By
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} the weights define a positive strong probability
on all subsets. To check a finite-coordinate cylinder, separate the specified
coordinates from the unspecified ones in \eqref{meas:eq:normalization-product}:
at a specified base coordinate the factor is $u_n$, at a specified rare
coordinate it is $u_nr_{n,a}=p_{n,a}$, and at an unspecified coordinate the
summed contribution is $u_n(1+\sum_ar_{n,a})=1$; the exceptional coordinates
satisfy the same marginal identity by \eqref{meas:eq:prob-rows}. The cylinder
mass is exactly \eqref{meas:eq:independent-cylinder}. Uniqueness follows from
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder}, and \eqref{meas:eq:product-weights} gives the
concentration on $\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A precise meaning of concentration]
The set $\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}}$ is countable, because the alphabets and $J$ are
finite and only finitely many other coordinates may vary; it is Borel.
Formula \eqref{meas:eq:product-weights} gives
$\mu(\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}})=0$ and $\mu(\Omega_{\mathrm{fin}})=1$. A
path with infinitely many deviations can still lie in each of its
positive-mass finite cylinders; it is simply not a point atom.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Bernoulli variables}

\begin{corollary}[One-line Bernoulli criterion; source 10]
\label{meas:cor:bernoulli}
Let $0\le p_n\le1$ in $\KR$ prescribe independent Bernoulli coordinates with
success probabilities $p_n$, and put $q_n=\min(p_n,1-p_n)$. There is a positive
strong Borel product law if and only if $(q_n)_{n\ge1}$ is strongly summable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A residue Bernoulli law is nondeterministic exactly when $\st(q_n)>0$. Since
every $q_n\ge0$, coefficient-local finiteness at exponent $0$ makes this happen
at only finitely many $n$ when $(q_n)$ is strong. Outside those indices $q_n$ is
exactly the rare-entry probability. Conversely, a strong rare-entry tail
together with finitely many exceptional $q_n$ is a strong family. Apply
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:independent}.
\end{proof}

Choose at each coordinate a state of probability $1-q_n$ as base state,
resolving ties arbitrarily. With the finitely many noninfinitesimal factors
separated before the infinite product is formed, the probability of the path
whose deviations occur exactly at a finite set $F$ is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:bernoulli-atom}
 \Bigl(\hprod_{n\ge1}(1-q_n)\Bigr)\prod_{n\in F}\frac{q_n}{1-q_n};
\end{equation}
the denominators never vanish, since $q_n\le\frac12$. In particular an i.i.d.\
Bernoulli law with real bias $p$ has a strong extension exactly when
$p\in\{0,1\}$ (compare Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:exchangeable}).

\begin{example}[Successes and failures of strong extension; source 10]
\label{meas:ex:bernoulli}
In $\R((t^\Q))$, the probabilities $p_n=t^n$ admit a strong product law;
its all-zero atom is
$\hprod_{n\ge1}(1-t^n)$. So do $p_n=t^{n/(n+1)}$, although the associated
partial sums need not converge in the valuation topology. Neither
$p_n=2^{-n}t$ nor $p_n=t$ admits one: infinitely many rare entries contribute at
exponent $1$, and in the first case this persists despite ordinary
summability of the real coefficients. The probabilities $p_n=t^{1/n}$ fail
because their supports descend, and $p_n=\frac12$ at infinitely many coordinates
fails already at the residue level. Finitely many ordinary nontrivial
probabilities together with a strong infinitesimal rare tail do admit an
extension.
\end{example}

At a deterministic baseline with perturbations on a common well-ordered
exponent set, Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:bernoulli} says that every exponent row
must be finitely supported; this is source~15's finite-row criterion, compared
with the $\ell^1$ threshold of the coefficientwise class in
Remark~\ref{meas:rem:rowfinite}.

\section{Coarse-graining can hide a genuine obstruction}\label{meas:sec:coarse}

Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:independent} tests the individual rare entries, not
merely their sum at each coordinate. This is necessary because positive Hahn
series can have negative higher coefficients. Take
\[
 \Gamma=\Q\oplus_{\mathrm{lex}}\Q,\qquad
 \alpha=(0,1),\quad\beta=(1,0),\quad \gamma_n=\beta+\tfrac1n\alpha,
\]
so that $\gamma_1>\gamma_2>\cdots$ while $n\alpha<\gamma_n$ for every $n$. For
$E_n=\{0,1,2\}$ put
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:three-state}
 p_{n,0}=1-t^{n\alpha},\qquad
 p_{n,1}=\tfrac12\bigl(t^{n\alpha}+t^{\gamma_n}\bigr),\qquad
 p_{n,2}=\tfrac12\bigl(t^{n\alpha}-t^{\gamma_n}\bigr).
\end{equation}
All three entries are positive and sum to $1$; the residue state is $0$ with
probability $1$.

\begin{theorem}[Extension may fail after refining a valid binary law;
source 10]\label{meas:thm:coarse}
The independent three-state cylinder laws \eqref{meas:eq:three-state} have no
positive strong Borel extension. Their binary coarse-graining, which merges
states $1$ and $2$, has a positive strong Borel extension.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The coarse-grained rare probability is $p_{n,1}+p_{n,2}=t^{n\alpha}$; this family
is strongly summable, so Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:bernoulli} gives a binary strong
product law. The individual rare-entry supports contain the strictly
decreasing sequence $(\gamma_n)$, so \eqref{meas:eq:rare-family} is not strongly
summable and Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:independent} rules out a three-state
extension.

There is also a direct witness. Let $B_n$ be the event that the first $n-1$
coordinates are $0$ and the $n$th is $1$. These events are pairwise disjoint,
with prescribed masses
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:direct-coarse}
 h_n=\tfrac12\bigl(t^{n\alpha}+t^{\gamma_n}\bigr)\prod_{k<n}\bigl(1-t^{k\alpha}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The coefficient of $t^{\gamma_n}$ in $h_n$ is $\frac12$: contributions from
$t^{n\alpha}$ have first coordinate $0$, and $t^{\gamma_n}$ times a nonempty
subproduct has exponent strictly larger than $\gamma_n$. So the combined
support of the $h_n$ contains the descending sequence $(\gamma_n)$, and this
disjoint family violates \eqref{meas:eq:SH}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why positivity does not protect support]
The negative coefficient of $t^{\gamma_n}$ in $p_{n,2}$ does not make that
probability negative, since the smaller exponent $n\alpha$ has positive
leading coefficient. Upon aggregation the two higher coefficients cancel; this
is exactly the information lost by testing only total rare probabilities. The
example does not contradict preservation of strong measures under pushforward;
it shows that a valid coarse law need not admit a prescribed strong
refinement.
\end{remark}

With $\iota(a,b)=a\omega+b$, which is additive and strictly order preserving on
$\Q\oplus_{\mathrm{lex}}\Q$, Convention~\ref{meas:conv:surreal} sends
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:surreal-coarse}
 t^{n\alpha}\mapsto\omega^{-n},\qquad t^{\gamma_n}\mapsto\omega^{-\omega-1/n},
\end{equation}
so \eqref{meas:eq:three-state} is an explicit system of positive surreal
probabilities, not merely a formal example in an unrelated field.

\begin{proposition}[Absoluteness under exponent enlargement; source 10]
\label{meas:prop:enlarge}
Let $j:\Gamma\to\Delta$ be an order-preserving additive embedding, and
transport coherent cylinder values from $k((t^\Gamma))$ to $k((u^\Delta))$ by
$t^\gamma\mapsto u^{j(\gamma)}$. A strong extension exists in the larger field if
and only if it exists in the original one. For real coefficients the same
holds for positive strong extensions.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The combined support is carried order-isomorphically onto its image, so (C1)
is unchanged; the widths at image exponents are unchanged and those at all
other exponents are zero, so (C2) is unchanged.
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder} gives the equivalence for signed or complex
extensions, and its uniqueness proof shows that an extension in the larger
field acquires no extra exponents, since a finite atomic coefficient measure
vanishing on every cylinder is zero. The reconstructed singleton weights are
exactly the transported original weights, and the ordered embedding preserves
their signs.
\end{proof}

In particular, allowing more surreal exponents does not rescue any failed
strong cylinder extension: a proposed surreal extension has set-sized range and
can be localized together with the original data in one larger exponent group
(Section~\ref{meas:sec:surreal}), where the proposition applies. Source~11 makes
the same observation for its moment example
(Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:actualNo}), and source~15 for the coefficientwise
i.i.d.\ obstruction (after Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:iid}).

\part{Strong Hahn measures: moments, hidden negative mass, and the
\texorpdfstring{$\omega+1$}{omega+1} threshold}\label{meas:part:moments}

This part is source~11's, with source~10's Cantor-space form of the hidden
negative atom and of the threshold. Sample points are \emph{ordinary real
points}; the values of measures, moments, polynomial coefficients and inner
products lie in a Hahn field or in the surreals. Only in the finite Gaussian
quadrature theorem are nodes allowed to lie in a real closed Hahn field.
Keeping these two sample domains apart is essential to the results.

\section{An exact strong moment representation criterion}\label{meas:sec:moments}

The classical moment problem asks when a sequence $(m_n)$ is represented by a
positive measure through $m_n=\int x^n\,d\mu$. On $[0,1]$, positivity of the
quadratic forms of squares and of $X(1-X)$ times squares suffices, and
equivalent conditions are nonnegative finite differences of the
moments~\cite[Theorems 1.16 and 1.18]{Schmudgen}. Replacing real values by Hahn
values changes two
separate things: one must say what an infinite sum means, and positivity
becomes lexicographic across infinitesimal scales. An ordinary polynomial has
finite degree even when its coefficients are surreal, so it may be unable to
tell a late infinitesimal negative weight from infinitely many earlier positive
weights. This part makes that obstruction exact.

\subsection{Finite scalar moment data and simple-root recurrences}

\begin{lemma}[Finite signed moment sequences]\label{meas:lem:prony}
Let $(a_n)_{n\ge0}$ be a real sequence and $\Omega\subseteq\R$. The following are
equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item There are finitely many distinct points $x_1,\ldots,x_r\in\Omega$ and real
coefficients $c_1,\ldots,c_r$ with
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:finiteexp}
 a_n=\sum_{j=1}^rc_jx_j^n\qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
\item A monic polynomial $r(Z)$ with distinct roots, all in $\Omega$,
annihilates the sequence under the forward shift: $r(E)a=0$, $(Ea)_n=a_{n+1}$.
The constant polynomial $r=1$ is allowed for the zero sequence.
\end{enumerate}
After zero coefficients are removed the representation \eqref{meas:eq:finiteexp}
is unique. We read $0^0=1$ in moment formulas.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For (i)$\Rightarrow$(ii) take $r(Z)=\prod_j(Z-x_j)$; each sequence $(x_j^n)$ is
killed by $E-x_j$, including $(1,0,0,\ldots)$ when $x_j=0$. Conversely, let $r$
have degree $r_0$ and distinct roots $x_1,\ldots,x_{r_0}$. Being monic, it
determines all terms from the first $r_0$; the Vandermonde matrix
$(x_j^n)_{0\le n<r_0,\,1\le j\le r_0}$ is invertible, so the $c_j$ can be solved
from those terms, and \eqref{meas:eq:finiteexp} then satisfies the same
recurrence and initial data. For uniqueness subtract two representations and
use the Vandermonde system on the union of their nodes.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, $\sum_{n\ge0}a_nz^n=\sum_jc_j/(1-x_jz)$ as formal power series,
a node $0$ contributing the constant $c_j$. A rational generating function or
finite Hankel rank would not suffice: repeated roots give sequences such as
$nb^{n-1}$ ($b\ne0$), the moments of the derivative functional $p\mapsto p'(b)$
rather than of a finite signed point measure. No positivity of the $c_j$ is
assumed.

\subsection{Assembling the coefficients into a measure}

\begin{theorem}[Exact strong moment criterion; source 11]
\label{meas:thm:momentcriterion}
Let $\Omega\subseteq\R$ carry a countably separating $\sigma$-algebra containing
its relative Borel sets. Let $(m_n)_{n\ge0}\subseteq\KR$ with $m_0=1$, and put
\[
 S_m=\bigcup_{n\ge0}\supp m_n,\qquad a_n(\gamma)=\coef{\gamma}{m_n}.
\]
There is a positive strong Hahn probability on $\Omega$ with
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:momentrep}
 m_n=\int_\Omega x^n\,d\mu(x)\qquad(n\ge0)
\end{equation}
if and only if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $S_m$ is well ordered;
\item for every $\gamma\in S_m$ the sequence $(a_n(\gamma))_{n\ge0}$ has a finite
signed representation
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:rows}
 a_n(\gamma)=\sum_{x\in F_\gamma}c_{\gamma,x}x^n,\qquad F_\gamma\subseteq\Omega\text{ finite},
\end{equation}
equivalently satisfies the simple-root recurrence of
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:prony} with roots in $\Omega$;
\item with $c_{\gamma,x}=0$ at missing nodes, every reassembled series
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:reassemble}
 w_x=\sum_{\gamma\in S_m}c_{\gamma,x}\,t^\gamma
\end{equation}
is nonnegative in $\KR$.
\end{enumerate}
The representing positive strong measure is unique. More generally,
conditions (a) and (b) alone give a unique \emph{signed} strong measure with
these moments, and (c) is exactly its positivity.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose a signed strong measure represents the moments.
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} supplies strongly summable point weights and finite
coefficient measures as in \eqref{meas:eq:coord-atomic}; taking coefficients in
the integral (Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:integration}) gives \eqref{meas:eq:rows}.
Let $S_w=\bigcup_x\supp w_x$; certainly $S_m\subseteq S_w$. Conversely, at an
exponent of $S_w$ the coefficient measure is a nonzero finite signed point
measure; if all its moments vanished, the Vandermonde argument of
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:prony} would make it zero. So $S_m=S_w$, which gives (a),
and the weights in \eqref{meas:eq:reassemble} are the singleton weights of the
measure; for a positive measure they are nonnegative, which gives (c).

Conversely, (a) makes every series \eqref{meas:eq:reassemble} a Hahn series,
and (b) says that at each exponent only finitely many of them have a nonzero
coefficient. So the weight family is strongly summable, and
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} gives a signed strong measure whose moments agree
with $m_n$ coefficientwise by \eqref{meas:eq:rows}; its total mass is $m_0=1$ and
(c) makes it positive. Any other signed strong representing measure has finite
coefficient measures with the same moment sequences, which
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:prony} identifies; this proves uniqueness, even without
positivity.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Compactness is not responsible for this uniqueness]
For ordinary real measures compact support is a familiar source of moment
determinacy. The uniqueness above holds on an arbitrary ordinary real sample
set, even an unbounded one, because every coefficient measure has \emph{finite}
support. It is a coefficientwise Vandermonde theorem, not an appeal to uniform
polynomial approximation or to a real representation theorem.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{How to use the criterion, and what is not algorithmic.} The
criterion separates three tasks: verify a common well order across the
moments; solve a finite atomic problem at each exponent; inspect the leading
coefficient of each reconstructed point weight. Positive Hankel matrices over
$\KR$ do not substitute for the last task; the example of
Section~\ref{meas:sec:pattern} satisfies much stronger positivity conditions and
fails exactly there. With explicit finite recurrence data at one exponent the
reconstruction is finite algebra (find the distinct real roots in $\Omega$,
solve the Vandermonde system, verify the recurrence). But the theorem quantifies
over all exponents and all moments: it is not a uniform decision algorithm
from numerical access to coefficients or from a finite list of moments, and
finitely many verified recurrence instances do not establish an infinite
recurrence.

\section{Finite visibility and valuation barriers}\label{meas:sec:visibility}

\subsection{When finite polynomial tests do detect signs}

For a signed strong measure with singleton weights $(w_x)$, the atoms
\emph{visible by scale} $\gamma$ are
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:visible}
 D_\gamma=\{x:w_x\ne0,\ v(w_x)\le\gamma\},
\end{equation}
the union of the finite coefficient supports $F_\eta$ over $\eta\le\gamma$. A
fixed coefficient row is finite, but $D_\gamma$ need not be.

\begin{theorem}[Finite visibility detects a negative atom; source 11]
\label{meas:thm:visibility}
Let $\mu$ be a signed strong Hahn measure on $\Omega\subseteq\R$ and
$L(P)=\int P\,d\mu$. Suppose $w_{x_0}<0$, put $\gamma=v(w_{x_0})$, and assume
$D_\gamma$ is finite. Then a real polynomial $p$ of degree at most
$\abs{D_\gamma}-1$ satisfies $L(p^2)<0$. Consequently, if every $D_\gamma$ is
finite, the following are equivalent: $\mu$ is positive; $L(p^2)\ge0$ for all
$p\in\R[X]$; $L(P^2)\ge0$ for all $P\in\KR[X]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take $p(X)=\prod_{y\in D_\gamma\setminus\{x_0\}}(X-y)$. Then $p(x_0)$ is a nonzero
real number, and every other atom that can contribute at an exponent at most
$\gamma$ is killed by $p$. The weight at $x_0$ has no coefficient below $\gamma$,
so
\[
 v(L(p^2))=\gamma,\qquad \coef{\gamma}{L(p^2)}=\coef{\gamma}{w_{x_0}}\,p(x_0)^2<0.
\]
A positive measure is nonnegative on squares by strong summation of
nonnegative values; conversely square positivity rules out every negative
weight by the first assertion, and Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} applies.
\end{proof}

The same isolation, with the indicator of a cylinder containing $x_0$ and no
other point of $D_\gamma$ in place of $p^2$, is source~10's proof of the
cylinder form of the threshold (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:threshold}(a)).

\begin{corollary}[A necessary condition for hidden negative mass]
\label{meas:cor:hidden-necessary}
If a signed strong measure is nonnegative on all real polynomial squares,
every negative atom has infinitely many distinct atoms of strictly smaller
valuation.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Only finitely many atoms have valuation exactly $\gamma$, since each contributes
a nonzero coefficient at $\gamma$. If a negative atom of valuation $\gamma$ had
only finitely many earlier atoms, $D_\gamma$ would be finite and
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:visibility} would produce a negative square.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The safe support range; source 11]\label{meas:cor:safesupport}
Let $S=\bigcup_x\supp w_x$. If each element of $S$ has only finitely many
predecessors in $S$, equivalently $\ot(S)\le\omega$, then positivity on real
polynomial squares implies positivity of the measure. In particular a
globally left-finite support, with $S\cap(-\infty,\gamma]$ finite for every
$\gamma\in\Gamma$, has this property.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For a proposed negative atom of valuation $\gamma\in S$, the exponents of $S$ at
or before $\gamma$ form a finite set, each with a finite coefficient row, so
$D_\gamma$ is finite and Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:visibility} applies.
\end{proof}

A support of order type $\omega$ may be bounded above in $\Gamma$ and need not
be globally left-finite; only the finite-predecessor condition at an actual
leading exponent is used. The assumption is global: requiring each
\emph{individual} point weight to have left-finite support does not make the
family left-finite. Conversely finite visibility is weaker than a global
left-finite support, since infinitely many exponents attached to one fixed
atom still give a finite $D_\gamma$. It is the number of spatially distinct
atoms visible at a scale that controls the interpolation detector.

\subsection{A barrier for every finite-degree polynomial}

Let $\nu$ be a positive strong measure with weights $u_x$ and fix
$\gamma\in\Gamma$. Its earlier atom set is
\[
 E_\gamma(\nu)=\{x:u_x>0,\ v(u_x)<\gamma\}.
\]
A signed strong measure $\tau$ \emph{starts at scale} $\gamma$ if every nonzero
singleton weight of $\tau$ has valuation at least $\gamma$; by
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} every coefficient of every weight of $\tau$ then
occurs at an exponent at least $\gamma$.

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial positivity barrier; source 11]\label{meas:thm:barrier}
Let $\Omega\subseteq\R$, let $\nu$ be a positive strong measure with
$E_\gamma(\nu)$ infinite, let
$\tau$ be any signed strong measure starting at scale $\gamma$, and put
$L(P)=\int P\,d\nu+\int P\,d\tau$ for $P\in\KR[X]$. For every nonzero polynomial
$P$ with $P(x)\ge0$ at every \emph{ordinary sample point} $x\in\Omega$,
$L(P)>0$. In particular $L(P^2)>0$ for every nonzero $P\in\KR[X]$. No sign
assumption is made on $\tau$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $\delta$ be the least valuation of the nonzero coefficients of $P$; the
coefficient of $t^\delta$ in $P$ is a nonzero ordinary real polynomial
$p_\delta$. At each real sample point $v(P(x))\ge\delta$, with equality where
$p_\delta(x)\ne0$. A nonzero real polynomial has finitely many real zeros, so
choose $x\in E_\gamma(\nu)$ with $p_\delta(x)\ne0$. Pointwise positivity gives
$P(x)>0$. All values $u_yP(y)$ are nonnegative, and the chosen one has valuation
$v(u_x)+\delta<\gamma+\delta$; Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra} gives
$\int P\,d\nu>0$ with $v(\int P\,d\nu)<\gamma+\delta$. Every product contributing
to $\int P\,d\tau$ has valuation at least $\gamma+\delta$, and so does its sum,
whatever cancellation occurs. The tail cannot change the sign of the earlier
leading term, so $L(P)>0$.
\end{proof}

The word ``barrier'' refers to a valuation inequality, not a topological limit.
A finite-degree leading polynomial can annihilate only finitely many distinct
early atoms, and allowing Hahn coefficients does not help: a finite-degree
polynomial still has a nonzero ordinary coefficient polynomial at its least
exponent.

\begin{corollary}[Hermitian and localizing forms; source 11]
\label{meas:cor:hermitianbarrier}
Suppose $\Omega\subseteq[0,1]$ and the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:barrier} hold. Extend $L$ complex-linearly to $\KC[X]$, and
let $P^*$ conjugate the coefficients of $P$ while fixing $X$. For $P\ne0$ the
four values
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:fourpositive}
 L(P^*P),\qquad L(XP^*P),\qquad L((1-X)P^*P),\qquad L(X(1-X)P^*P)
\end{equation}
are strictly positive elements of $\KR$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At real $x$, $P^*(x)P(x)$ is a nonnegative real Hahn value. Writing $P=A+iB$,
$P^*P=A^2+B^2$ is a nonzero polynomial in $\KR[X]$, and the four polynomials
inside $L$ are nonzero and nonnegative on $\Omega$. Apply
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:barrier} to each.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Zariski-dense early atoms; source 11]\label{meas:cor:multivariate}
For $\Omega\subseteq\R^d$, replace the infinitude of $E_\gamma(\nu)$ by the
condition that no nonzero real polynomial vanishes on all of $E_\gamma(\nu)$.
Then Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:barrier} holds, with the same proof, for
$\KR[X_1,\ldots,X_d]$ and polynomials nonnegative on $\Omega$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The least-valuation coefficient of a nonzero multivariate Hahn polynomial is a
nonzero ordinary polynomial, and the condition supplies an early atom where it
does not vanish; the rest of the proof is unchanged.
\end{proof}

This is the multivariate replacement for ``a polynomial has finitely many
roots''. Infinitely many atoms on one algebraic curve would not suffice in
higher dimension, and the corollary is a sufficient condition, not a
classification of the intermediate cases.

\subsection{A topological barrier for continuous functions}

When the tail atoms lie in the ordinary closure of the earlier atom set, all
nonnegative continuous functions can fail to see a negative event.

\begin{theorem}[Continuous-test barrier; source 11]\label{meas:thm:continuousbarrier}
Let $\Omega$ be a metrizable space with a countably separating measurable
structure containing its Borel sets. Let $\nu$ be a positive strong Hahn
measure, let $\tau$ start at scale $\gamma$, and assume
$\{x:\tau(\{x\})\ne0\}\subseteq\overline{E_\gamma(\nu)}$. Then
$T(f)=\int f\,d\nu+\int f\,d\tau$ is nonnegative for every ordinary continuous
$f:\Omega\to\R$ with $f\ge0$. If $\Omega$ is compact and $T(1)=1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:orderbound}
 -\norm f_\infty\le T(f)\le\norm f_\infty\qquad(f\in C(\Omega,\R)).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $f(x)>0$ for some $x\in E_\gamma(\nu)$, the positive integral $\int f\,d\nu$ has
valuation less than $\gamma$, whereas $v(\int f\,d\tau)\ge\gamma$. Otherwise $f$
vanishes on $E_\gamma(\nu)$, hence on its closure and at every atom of $\tau$, and
$T(f)=\int f\,d\nu\ge0$. On a compact space apply positivity to $M\pm f$ with
$M=\norm f_\infty\in\R$.
\end{proof}

The bound \eqref{meas:eq:orderbound} is an ordered-field inequality with an
ordinary real sup norm. It does not make the target a real Banach space and does
not assert continuity into the valuation topology or the surreal fine
topology.

\section{One weight pattern, two test classes}\label{meas:sec:pattern}

Sources~10 and~11 found, independently, the same weights. We print them once.

\begin{definition}[The hidden-negative weight pattern; sources 10 and 11]
\label{meas:def:pattern}
Let $0<\delta_1<\delta_2<\cdots$ in $\Gamma$ and $\gamma\in\Gamma$ with
$\delta_n<\gamma$ for all $n$. For distinct points $a$ (the \emph{anchor}), $o$
(the \emph{negative atom}) and $e_1,e_2,\ldots$ (the \emph{early atoms}) of a set
$X$, the pattern is the weight family
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:pattern}
 w_{e_n}=t^{\delta_n},\qquad w_o=-t^\gamma,\qquad
 w_a=1-\hsum_{n\ge1}t^{\delta_n}+t^\gamma,
\end{equation}
with all other weights zero. The \emph{standard pattern} has $\Gamma=\Q$,
$\delta_n=q_n:=n/(n+1)$ and $\gamma=1$.
\end{definition}

At each $\delta_n$ exactly two weights ($e_n$ and $a$) have a nonzero
coefficient, at $\gamma$ two ($o$ and $a$), and at $0$ one ($a$). So the family is
strongly summable, with sum $1$ and global support
\[
 \{0\}\cup\{\delta_n:n\ge1\}\cup\{\gamma\},\qquad\text{of order type }\omega+1.
\]
The anchor has leading term $1$ and is positive, every early weight is positive,
and $w_o<0$. By Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} the pattern defines a normalized
strong signed measure on all subsets of $X$, with mass $-t^\gamma$ at $o$.

\subsection{On Cantor space, with cylinder tests (source 10)}

On binary Cantor space $\{0,1\}^{\N}$ take the anchor $a=(1,1,1,\ldots)$, the
negative atom $o=(0,0,0,\ldots)$, and the early atoms $e_n$, the path with its
single $1$ at position $n$. Let $\mu_{\mathrm C}$ be the strong signed measure of
the pattern.

\begin{proposition}[Every finite-dimensional law is nonnegative; source 10]
\label{meas:prop:hidden-positive}
For every cylinder $[s]$, $\mu_{\mathrm C}([s])\ge0$. Nevertheless no positive
strong measure has these cylinder values.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $N\ge1$ the all-zero cylinder $[0^N]$ contains $o$ and exactly the $e_n$
with $n>N$, but not $a$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:zero-cylinder}
 \mu_{\mathrm C}([0^N])=\hsum_{n>N}t^{\delta_n}-t^\gamma>0,
\end{equation}
with leading exponent $\delta_{N+1}<\gamma$ and coefficient $1$. A cylinder not
containing $o$ contains at most one $e_n$; if it also contains $a$, its mass has
constant coefficient $1$ and is positive, and otherwise its mass is one
$t^{\delta_n}$ or zero. The empty-word cylinder has mass $1$. So all cylinders,
and all finite unions of cylinders, are nonnegative. The unique strong signed
extension is $\mu_{\mathrm C}$ by Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder}, and
$\mu_{\mathrm C}(\{o\})=-t^\gamma<0$.
\end{proof}

The widths are at most two at every exponent. The failure is not unbounded
width, lack of a common support, or a negative finite law. Each finite
neighbourhood of $o$ receives a positive contribution at an exponent below
$\gamma$, but these contributions escape to later and later paths, and the
singleton has none of them.

\subsection{On the interval, with polynomial and continuous tests (source 11)}

From here through Section~\ref{meas:sec:hermitian} take the standard pattern on
$\Omega=[0,1]\subseteq\R$ with
\[
 a=\tfrac34,\qquad o=0,\qquad e_n=x_n:=\frac1{n+1},\qquad q_n=\frac n{n+1}\quad(n\ge1).
\]
The points $a,0,x_1,x_2,\ldots$ are distinct, and $0<q_1<q_2<\cdots<1$. The
associated signed strong measure on all subsets of $[0,1]$ is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:mustar}
 \mu_*=\Bigl(1-\hsum_{n\ge1}t^{q_n}+t\Bigr)\delta_a
 +\hsum_{n\ge1}t^{q_n}\delta_{x_n}-t\,\delta_0 ,
\end{equation}
a coefficientwise-finite strong sum of point measures, involving no countable
additivity in a real norm. Split it as
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:nutau}
 \nu=\Bigl(1-\hsum_{n\ge1}t^{q_n}\Bigr)\delta_a+\hsum_{n\ge1}t^{q_n}\delta_{x_n},
 \qquad \tau=t(\delta_a-\delta_0),\qquad \mu_*=\nu+\tau .
\end{equation}
Here $\nu$ is a positive strong probability, $\tau$ starts at scale $1$ and has
total mass zero, $E_1(\nu)=\{a,x_1,x_2,\ldots\}$ and $0\in\overline{E_1(\nu)}$ in
the \emph{ordinary} topology of the interval. For $P\in\KR[X]$, integration
against $\mu_*$ is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:explicitL}
 L(P)=P(a)+\hsum_{n\ge1}t^{q_n}\bigl(P(x_n)-P(a)\bigr)+t\bigl(P(a)-P(0)\bigr);
\end{equation}
for Hahn coefficients the middle sum is still strong by
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}. The moments are $m_0=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:mk}
 m_k=a^k+\hsum_{n\ge1}(x_n^k-a^k)\,t^{q_n}+a^k\,t\qquad(k\ge1),
\end{equation}
with the coefficient measures read off directly:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Exponent & Coefficient measure & Moments at that exponent\\
\midrule
$0$ & $\delta_a$ & $a^k$\\
$q_n$ & $\delta_{x_n}-\delta_a$ & $x_n^k-a^k$\\
$1$ & $\delta_a-\delta_0$ & $a^k-0^k$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
With $0^0=1$, every nonzero-exponent coefficient measure has total mass zero. At
$q_n$ the coefficient sequence is annihilated by $(E-x_n)(E-a)$, at $1$ by
$E(E-a)$: simple-root recurrences. So the failure below is not caused by a
complicated coefficient moment problem, a multiple root, or an unbounded
number of coefficient atoms.

\begin{theorem}[Full positivity but no positive strong representation;
source 11]\label{meas:thm:example}
The functional \eqref{meas:eq:explicitL} has the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $L(1)=1$, and $L(P)>0$ for every nonzero $P\in\KR[X]$ with $P(x)\ge0$ at
every ordinary $x\in[0,1]$.
\item Its complex-linear extension has all four values \eqref{meas:eq:fourpositive}
strictly positive, for every nonzero $P\in\KC[X]$ of every finite degree.
\item Its restriction to real-coefficient polynomials extends, by the same
formula, to a positive unital $\R$-linear functional
$T:C([0,1],\R)\to\KR$ satisfying the order bound \eqref{meas:eq:orderbound}.
\item There is no positive strong Hahn measure on the ordinary Borel space
$[0,1]$ with moment sequence $(m_k)$: the unique signed strong representing
measure is $\mu_*$, and $\mu_*(\{0\})=-t<0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The support and finite coefficient activity were checked after
Definition~\ref{meas:def:pattern}. By \eqref{meas:eq:nutau} the construction falls
under Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:barrier} with $\gamma=1$, since infinitely many
distinct atoms have positive weights of valuation less than $1$; this gives (i),
and Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:hermitianbarrier} gives (ii). The atoms $a$ and $0$ of
$\tau$ lie in the ordinary closure of the earlier atom set, so
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:continuousbarrier} gives (iii); \eqref{meas:eq:explicitL}
makes sense for every ordinary continuous function by
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:integration}. Finally
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:momentcriterion} gives uniqueness among signed strong
measures. Directly: at exponent $1$ the coefficient measure must have all the
moments of $\delta_a-\delta_0$, so finite coefficient atomicity and Vandermonde
uniqueness force it; at exponents below $1$ the coefficient measures in the table
have no atom at $0$. So the weight at $0$ has leading coefficient $-1$ at exponent
$1$ and cannot be nonnegative.
\end{proof}

The fourth conclusion concerns the strong class on the stated ordinary sample
space. It does not rule out generalized-limit probabilities, weaker
additivity, representations with a different target, or measures on a sample
space of nonreal field elements.

\begin{corollary}[All Hausdorff inequalities hold strictly; source 11]
\label{meas:cor:hausdorff}
For all ordinary integers $k,n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:hausdorff}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^j\binom nj m_{k+j}=L\bigl(X^k(1-X)^n\bigr)>0.
\end{equation}
In particular $1=m_0>m_1>m_2>\cdots>0$ and $\st(m_k)=a^k$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$X^k(1-X)^n$ is nonzero and nonnegative on $[0,1]$, so
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example}(i) applies; $n=1$ and $n=0$ give the stated
inequalities, and the standard part is read from \eqref{meas:eq:mk}.
\end{proof}

For real sequences the non-strict finite-difference conditions characterize
interval moment sequences~\cite{Schmudgen}. Here they hold at every order and
still produce no positive \emph{strong Hahn} measure. This is an obstruction to
one non-Archimedean extension of a representation theorem, not a counterexample
to the classical real theorem.

\paragraph{A continuous detector sequence and the missing limit.} For integers
$k\ge2$ let $f_k(x)=\max(1-kx,0)$ on $[0,1]$. These nonnegative continuous
functions decrease pointwise to $\ind_{\{0\}}$, with $f_k(a)=0$ and $f_k(x_n)>0$
exactly for $n\ge k$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:tent}
 T(f_k)=\hsum_{n\ge k}\Bigl(1-\frac k{n+1}\Bigr)t^{q_n}-t,\qquad
 v(T(f_k))=q_k,\qquad \lc(T(f_k))=\frac1{k+1}>0.
\end{equation}
The limiting indicator, integrated against $\mu_*$, gives $-t$. There is no
contradiction: the positive terms do not become small relative to the scale
$t$, since $T(f_k)-\mu_*(\{0\})$ has valuation $q_k<1$ for every $k$, and a
monotone-convergence step across this barrier is unavailable. The ordinary
topology is what matters: a nonnegative continuous function positive at $0$ is
positive at infinitely many $x_n$, and one vanishing at every $x_n$ vanishes at
$0$.

\paragraph{Why finite truncations are not proofs of the example.} Let $\mu_N$
retain only the early atoms with $1\le n\le N$, with the anchor adjusted. It is
normalized and still has mass $-t$ at $0$, but
$p_N(X)=(X-a)\prod_{n=1}^N(X-x_n)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:finitefailure}
 \int p_N^2\,d\mu_N=-t\,p_N(0)^2<0.
\end{equation}
Once the next atom is included the same polynomial meets an earlier positive
contribution:
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:restore}
 v\Bigl(\int p_N^2\,d\mu_{N+1}\Bigr)=q_{N+1},\qquad
 \lc\Bigl(\int p_N^2\,d\mu_{N+1}\Bigr)=p_N(x_{N+1})^2>0.
\end{equation}
Every finite truncation eventually fails; the infinite theorem says that every
fixed finite-degree polynomial meets a surviving earlier atom. Reversing those
quantifiers would invalidate the proof. Source~11's check suite tests both
\eqref{meas:eq:finitefailure} and \eqref{meas:eq:restore}, and source~10's suite
tests the corresponding cutoff for $\mu_{\mathrm C}$, to make the distinction
visible.

\begin{remark}[The interval example fails in the coefficientwise class too]
\label{meas:rem:mustar-coef}
No positive \emph{coefficientwise} Hahn measure on $[0,1]$ has the moments
\eqref{meas:eq:mk} either. A finite signed Borel measure on $[0,1]$ is determined
by its moments (Weierstrass approximation and uniqueness in the Riesz
representation), so every coefficientwise representing measure has exactly the
coefficient measures of the table, and is $\mu_*$ read coefficientwise. The
null-ideal criterion (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:nullideal}) detects the failure:
$\{0\}$ is null for $\abs{\mu_0}=\delta_a$ and for every
$\abs{\mu_{q_n}}=\delta_{x_n}+\delta_a$, so $\{0\}\in\mathcal J_{<1}$, while
$\mu_1^-(\{0\})=1>0$. (This remark is added in the merge; neither source states
it.)
\end{remark}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$\omega+1$}{omega+1} threshold in the strong class}
\label{meas:sec:threshold}

\begin{definition}[Finite initial segments]
A well-ordered set $S$ has \emph{finite initial segments} if
$\{\delta\in S:\delta\le\gamma\}$ is finite for every $\gamma\in S$; equivalently
$\ot(S)\le\omega$.
\end{definition}

This is an internal condition on the active support and does not require $S$ to
be unbounded in $\Gamma$: $\{n/(n+1):n\ge1\}$ has finite initial segments though
it is bounded in $\Q$. The pattern of Definition~\ref{meas:def:pattern} has the
smallest possible infinite support order type that can exhibit hidden negative
mass: an increasing sequence followed by one later exponent. Sources~10 and~11
proved this for two test classes; the threshold is the same, and it is stated
once.

\begin{theorem}[The $\omega+1$ threshold for strong measures; sources 10
and 11]\label{meas:thm:threshold}
\hfill
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Cylinder tests} (source 10). Let $S\subseteq\Gamma_{\ge0}$ be well
ordered with $0\in S$. The following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $S$ has finite initial segments.
\item On binary Cantor space, every normalized strong real signed measure with
global support contained in $S$ and nonnegative cylinder masses is positive on
every Borel set.
\item Every normalized nonnegative coherent binary cylinder family with
combined support contained in $S$ and satisfying \eqref{meas:eq:C2} has a positive
strong extension.
\end{enumerate}
For (i)$\Rightarrow$(ii) the binary alphabet may be replaced by arbitrary finite
alphabets.
\item \emph{Polynomial tests} (source 11). Among signed strong Hahn measures on
ordinary real sample sets whose polynomial moment functional is nonnegative on
every real polynomial square but which have a negative event, the least
possible order type of the global coefficient support is $\omega+1$. It is
attained by $\mu_*$, even with the stronger positivity properties of
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(a) Assume (i) and let a strong signed measure $\mu$ have nonnegative cylinder
masses. Suppose some $w_x<0$ and let $\gamma=v(w_x)$. There are finitely many
active exponents $\delta\le\gamma$, each with finitely many coefficient atoms, so
their union $F$ is finite. Choose a cylinder containing $x$ and no other point
of $F$; its coefficients at all exponents at most $\gamma$ agree with those of
$w_x$, and there are no exponents outside $S$. So its leading coefficient is the
negative coefficient of $w_x$ at $\gamma$, contradicting cylinder positivity.
Every point weight is nonnegative, and Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic} gives (ii);
the argument works for any finite alphabets. The equivalence
(ii)$\Leftrightarrow$(iii) follows from Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:cylinder}: (C1) is
automatic for a subset of $S$ and the strong extension is unique. If (i) fails,
choose $\gamma\in S$ with infinitely many predecessors in $S$; since $S$ is well
ordered it contains a strictly increasing sequence
$0<\delta_1<\delta_2<\cdots<\gamma$. The pattern with these exponents has support in
$S$, is normalized and strong, and by
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:hidden-positive} has nonnegative cylinders and a
negative singleton; so (ii) fails.

(b) If the global support has order type below $\omega+1$, its order type is at
most $\omega$, and Corollary~\ref{meas:cor:safesupport} makes every singleton
weight, hence (Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:atomic}) every event, nonnegative. The
measure $\mu_*$ has global support of order type exactly $\omega+1$, positive
polynomial squares by Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example}, and a negative singleton.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the threshold is and is not]\label{meas:rem:threshold-scope}
The proof of the positive direction is not an order-completeness argument: it
does not pass to an infimum of positive test values, but isolates finitely many
coefficient atoms, and the pattern shows exactly why this finite isolation fails
when an active exponent has infinitely many predecessors. The order type
$\omega+1$ is that of the \emph{global} coefficient support of the measure, which
by the proof of Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:momentcriterion} is also the union of the
supports of all its moments; it does not assert that a single moment of support
type $\omega$ causes or precludes the phenomenon. It is a normal-form
\emph{support} order type, not a Conway birthday or sign-sequence length of an
individual number. Finally, the theorem quantifies over strong measures only:
in the coefficientwise class two exponents suffice
(Example~\ref{meas:ex:negative}, Section~\ref{meas:sub:traps}). A formalization
must keep the data separate (coherence, well order of the combined support,
boundedness of each width, signs of reconstructed point weights); collapsing
the last into cylinder positivity would encode the false assertion refuted by
Proposition~\ref{meas:prop:hidden-positive}.
\end{remark}

\section{Exact Hankel determinants and recurrence scales}
\label{meas:sec:determinants}

\subsection{A strongly summable Cauchy--Binet identity}

For a finite set $F$ of distinct ordinary real points write
$V(F)^2=\prod_{\{x,y\}\subseteq F}(x-y)^2$, independent of any ordering, and
equal to $1$ when $\abs F\le1$. For an ordinary real polynomial $g$ put
\[
 H_d^{(g)}=\bigl(L(g(X)X^{i+j})\bigr)_{0\le i,j\le d},\qquad
 \Delta_d^{(g)}=\det H_d^{(g)},
\]
and omit the superscript when $g=1$.

\begin{lemma}[Strong atomic determinant formula; source 11]\label{meas:lem:CB}
For any signed strong Hahn measure on an ordinary real sample set,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:CB}
 \Delta_d^{(g)}=\hsum_{\substack{F\subseteq\Omega\\\abs F=d+1}}
 \Bigl(\prod_{x\in F}w_xg(x)\Bigr)V(F)^2,
\end{equation}
and the displayed family is strongly summable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Multiplying weights by the real values $g(x)$ preserves strong summability;
products of any fixed $d+1$ members are strongly summable by
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}, and multiplication by the real number $V(F)^2$
adds no exponent. With $v_x=(1,x,\ldots,x^d)^{\mathsf T}$ one has
$H_d^{(g)}=\hsum_xw_xg(x)v_xv_x^{\mathsf T}$ coefficientwise. Expand the determinant
multilinearly in its $d+1$ columns; terms with a repeated sample point vanish,
and grouping the remaining ordered choices by their underlying set $F$ gives
one Vandermonde determinant and its transpose, that is $V(F)^2$. This is the
finite Cauchy--Binet calculation, valid coefficientwise because every product
family involved is strongly summable.
\end{proof}

The finite Cauchy--Binet mechanism is classical. What matters here is
licensing the infinite atomic expansion and using its \emph{unique}
lowest-valuation term although one weight is negative.

\subsection{The complete leading-term calculation}

For $d\ge0$ put
\[
 Y_d=\{a,x_1,\ldots,x_d\},\qquad s_d=\sum_{j=1}^dq_j,\qquad C_d=V(Y_d)^2,
\]
with $Y_0=\{a\}$, $s_0=0$, $C_0=1$, and let $\mathcal H_r=\sum_{j=1}^r1/j$.

\begin{theorem}[Every determinant scale and leading coefficient; source 11]
\label{meas:thm:determinants}
For the functional of Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example},
\begin{align}
 v(\Delta_d)&=s_d=d+1-\mathcal H_{d+1},\label{meas:eq:detval}\\
 \lc(\Delta_d)&=C_d=\Biggl[
 \frac{\prod_{j=1}^{d}(3j-1)\,\prod_{j=1}^{d}(j-1)!}{4^d\bigl((d+1)!\bigr)^d}
 \Biggr]^2>0.\label{meas:eq:detlc}
\end{align}
For each of $g=X$, $1-X$ and $X(1-X)$,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:localdet}
 v(\Delta_d^{(g)})=s_d,\qquad \lc(\Delta_d^{(g)})=C_d\prod_{y\in Y_d}g(y)>0.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The valuations of the nonzero point weights are, in increasing order,
$0,q_1,q_2,\ldots,1$, the last belonging to the negative atom at $0$. Among all
sets of $d+1$ distinct atoms, $Y_d$ uniquely minimizes the sum of valuations,
and its product of leading weight coefficients is $1$; replacing any of these
earliest valuations by a later one strictly increases the sum. So
\eqref{meas:eq:CB} has a unique lowest-valuation term, of valuation $s_d$ and
leading coefficient $V(Y_d)^2$. The valuation sum is
$\sum_{j=1}^d(1-\frac1{j+1})=d+1-\mathcal H_{d+1}$. For the Vandermonde product
use $a-x_j=\frac{3j-1}{4(j+1)}$ and $x_i-x_j=\frac{j-i}{(i+1)(j+1)}$ for
$1\le i<j\le d$: the numerators of the latter multiply to
$\prod_{j=1}^d(j-1)!$, their denominators to $((d+1)!)^{d-1}$, and the anchor
differences contribute another $4^d(d+1)!$; squaring gives
\eqref{meas:eq:detlc}. Each listed localizer is a positive real number at every
point of $Y_d$ and leaves the corresponding valuations unchanged; elsewhere it
can remove a contribution but not create an earlier one. The same unique
minimum proves \eqref{meas:eq:localdet}.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rll@{}}
\toprule
$d$ & $v(\Delta_d)$ & $\lc(\Delta_d)$\\
\midrule
0 & $0$ & $1$\\
1 & $1/2$ & $1/16$\\
2 & $7/6$ & $25/82944$\\
3 & $23/12$ & $25/764411904$\\
4 & $163/60$ & $121/3057647616000000$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The valuations can exceed the location $1$ of the negative atom. There is no
inconsistency: a determinant uses a product of $d+1$ weights, and for each fixed
size its earliest product uses the earliest $d+1$ atoms and avoids the negative
one.

\subsection{Orthogonal polynomials and loss of a common support}

Because $L$ is positive on nonzero squares there is a unique monic orthogonal
polynomial $\pi_d$ of each degree $d$, with $h_d=L(\pi_d^2)>0$. With
$\Delta_{-1}=1$, finite Gram elimination (a unit triangular change of basis from
monomials) gives
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:normratio}
 h_d=\frac{\Delta_d}{\Delta_{d-1}},\qquad \Delta_d=\prod_{j=0}^dh_j .
\end{equation}
The monic three-term recurrence is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:three}
 X\pi_d=\pi_{d+1}+\alpha_d\pi_d+\beta_d\pi_{d-1},\qquad
 \beta_d=\frac{h_d}{h_{d-1}}>0\quad(d\ge1);
\end{equation}
lower terms vanish by orthogonality (move $X$ to the other factor), and pairing
with $\pi_{d-1}$ identifies $\beta_d$.

\begin{corollary}[Explicit Jacobi scales; source 11]\label{meas:cor:jacobi}
For $d\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:jacobivals}
 v(h_d)=q_d=\frac d{d+1},\qquad v(\beta_d)=\frac1{d(d+1)}.
\end{equation}
The off-diagonal coefficient $\sqrt{\beta_d}$ of the orthonormal Jacobi matrix has
valuation $1/(2d(d+1))$. Each $\beta_d$ is a Hahn series, but the family
$(\beta_d)_{d\ge1}$ has no common well-ordered support and is not strongly
summable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By \eqref{meas:eq:normratio} and Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:determinants},
$v(h_d)=s_d-s_{d-1}=q_d$; with $h_0=1$ and $q_0=0$,
$v(\beta_d)=q_d-q_{d-1}=1/(d(d+1))$. These leading exponents form a strictly
decreasing sequence inside the union of the supports of the $\beta_d$.
\end{proof}

This is a concrete warning about infinite orthogonalization. The moment family
has a common well-ordered support and every finite Gram process is valid, but
the sequence of normalized recurrence data loses the common support. An
infinite Jacobi matrix can still be defined entrywise; what fails is a
description in which all its entries share one well-ordered Hahn support. No
assertion about every other operator representation is intended.

\section{Positive Gaussian quadrature at every finite order}
\label{meas:sec:quadrature}

The next theorem separates a finite algebraic success from the infinite
strong-measure obstruction. Its mechanism is classical orthogonal-polynomial and
spectral algebra, not a new spectral theorem. Source~11 reproved the finite
spectral theorem over a real closed field for it; the collection already
contains that theorem, as \path{thm:spectral} of the spectral-theory
report~\cite{repospec} for any set-sized real closed field $F$ (every symmetric
matrix over $F$ has an orthonormal eigenbasis over $F$ with eigenvalues in $F$),
and as \path{spec:thm:hermitian} for Hermitian matrices over $\C((t^\Gamma))$
at \emph{any} set-sized $\Gamma$, with a real orthogonal diagonalizer for real
symmetric input. We cite them instead of reproving. Real closedness of $F$ is
used below also for the square roots in the orthonormalization; for
$F=\R((t^\Gamma))$ this holds when $\Gamma$ is divisible.

\begin{theorem}[Finite Gaussian quadrature over a real closed field;
source 11]\label{meas:thm:gauss}
Let $F$ be a real closed ordered field and $L:F[X]\to F$ an $F$-linear map with
$L(1)=1$ such that for every nonzero $p\in F[X]$
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:gausshyp}
 L(p^2)>0,\qquad L(Xp^2)>0,\qquad L((1-X)p^2)>0.
\end{equation}
For each ordinary integer $N\ge1$ there are distinct $z_1,\ldots,z_N\in F$ with
$0<z_j<1$ and positive weights $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_N\in F$ summing to one
such that
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:gauss}
 L(f)=\sum_{j=1}^N\lambda_jf(z_j)\qquad(\deg f\le2N-1).
\end{equation}
The nodes are the roots of the monic orthogonal polynomial $\pi_N$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Equip $F[X]$ with $\inner pq=L(pq)$. Positive definiteness and square roots in $F$
permit finite Gram--Schmidt; let $e_0=1,e_1,\ldots,e_{N-1}$ be orthonormal
polynomials of the indicated degrees with positive leading coefficients
$\kappa_j$. Compressing multiplication by $X$ to their span gives the symmetric
matrix $J_{ij}=L(Xe_ie_j)$, $0\le i,j<N$. It is tridiagonal (move $X$ to the
lower-degree factor), with off-diagonal entries $\kappa_j/\kappa_{j+1}>0$. For a
nonzero vector $c$ with $p=\sum c_je_j$,
$c^{\mathsf T}Jc=L(Xp^2)>0$ and $c^{\mathsf T}(I-J)c=L((1-X)p^2)>0$. By the finite
spectral theorem over $F$ (\path{thm:spectral} of~\cite{repospec}), $J$ has an
orthonormal eigenbasis over $F$ with eigenvalues in $F$, and these two
inequalities put all eigenvalues in $(0,1)_F$. An eigenvector of an irreducible tridiagonal matrix
is determined by its first coordinate, so each eigenspace has dimension at most
one; $J$ being diagonalizable, its $N$ eigenvalues are distinct and every
normalized eigenvector has nonzero first coordinate. Choose an orthonormal
eigenbasis $u_1,\ldots,u_N$ with eigenvalues $z_j$ and put $\lambda_j=(u_j)_0^2>0$;
orthonormality gives $\sum_j\lambda_j=1$. Expanding the characteristic
determinants of the leading principal submatrices gives the monic recurrence
\eqref{meas:eq:three} with the same initial data $1$ and $X-\alpha_0$, so
$\det(XI-J)=\pi_N(X)$ and the eigenvalues are the roots of $\pi_N$. Write $\mathbf e_0$ for the first standard coordinate vector (the
coefficient vector of the polynomial $e_0=1$). For $0\le k<N$,
$J^k\mathbf e_0$ is the coefficient vector of $X^k$: every intermediate
power still has degree less than $N$, so compression loses no term.
Consequently $L(r)=\mathbf e_0^{\mathsf T}r(J)\mathbf e_0
=\sum_j\lambda_jr(z_j)$ for $\deg r<N$. For $\deg f\le2N-1$ write $f=\pi_Nq+r$ with $\deg
q,\deg r<N$; orthogonality gives $L(\pi_Nq)=0$ and $\pi_N(z_j)=0$ gives
$f(z_j)=r(z_j)$.
\end{proof}

For $F=\R((t^\Q))$ and the example, all hypotheses hold by
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example}, so every finite quadrature problem has a
solution with positive weights. This does not produce a strong measure on the
ordinary sample space: the nodes lie in $F$, not necessarily in $\R$.

\begin{lemma}[Orthogonal polynomial from atomic subsets; source 11]
\label{meas:lem:heine}
For a signed strong atomic measure with $\Delta_{N-1}\ne0$, the monic polynomial
orthogonal to all polynomials of degree less than $N$ is
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:heine}
 \pi_N(X)=\frac1{\Delta_{N-1}}\hsum_{\substack{F\subseteq\Omega\\\abs F=N}}
 \Bigl(\prod_{x\in F}w_x\Bigr)V(F)^2\prod_{x\in F}(X-x),
\end{equation}
with all coefficient sums strong.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Strong summability follows from fixed finite products of the weight family,
since every coefficient of $\prod_{x\in F}(X-x)$ is an ordinary real number. The
numerator has leading coefficient $\Delta_{N-1}$ by Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:CB}, so the
polynomial is monic. Multiply the numerator by $X^k$, $0\le k<N$, and apply $L$:
this adds a sample point $y$; terms with $y\in F$ vanish, and grouping the rest by
the $(N+1)$-point union $G=F\cup\{y\}$ gives
\[
 \Bigl(\prod_{x\in G}w_x\Bigr)V(G)^2\sum_{y\in G}
 \frac{y^k}{\prod_{x\in G\setminus\{y\}}(y-x)} ,
\]
whose last sum is the coefficient of $X^N$ in the Lagrange interpolation of $X^k$
at the $N+1$ nodes of $G$, hence zero since $k<N$. All groupings are strong by
Lemma~\ref{meas:lem:sumalgebra}. The linear system for the lower coefficients of
a monic orthogonal polynomial has matrix $H_{N-1}$, with nonzero determinant.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Standard parts and nonreal quadrature nodes; source 11]
\label{meas:thm:quadratureresidues}
For the example,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:pistandard}
 \st(\pi_N(X))=(X-a)\prod_{n=1}^{N-1}(X-x_n),
\end{equation}
every coefficient of $\pi_N$ being finite and the standard part being taken
coefficientwise. The $N$ Gaussian nodes can be labelled so that their standard
parts are $a,x_1,\ldots,x_{N-1}$. Every node lies in $(0,a)_F$; in particular the
node with standard part $a$ is not an ordinary real number, for every $N$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In \eqref{meas:eq:heine} the unique lowest-valuation subset is $Y_{N-1}$, as in
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:determinants}. The denominator has valuation $s_{N-1}$ and
leading coefficient $C_{N-1}$; every coefficient of the numerator has valuation
at least $s_{N-1}$, with coefficient $C_{N-1}\prod_{y\in Y_{N-1}}(X-y)$ there.
Division gives finite coefficients and \eqref{meas:eq:pistandard}. The nodes lie
between $0$ and $1$, and taking standard parts in $\pi_N(X)=\prod_j(X-z_j)$ shows
that the multiset of their standard parts is the root multiset of
\eqref{meas:eq:pistandard}: $N$ distinct ordinary points. For the upper bound,
view the signed measure as supported on $[0,a]$; its earlier positive atoms are
still an infinite subset of that interval, so Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:barrier}
gives $L((a-X)p^2)>0$ for $p\ne0$, that is, $aI-J$ is positive definite in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:gauss}, and every $z_j<a$. An ordinary real number
equals its standard part, so the node with standard part $a$ would be $a$
itself, which is excluded.
\end{proof}

At $N=1$ the quadrature has weight $1$ and node
$m_1=a+\hsum_n(x_n-a)t^{q_n}+at$, infinitesimally below $a$. For larger $N$ the
residue nodes successively include more early atoms. This is a finite
phenomenon, not an assertion that the finite quadratures converge to a positive
strong measure on ordinary points.

\section{Surcomplex Hermitian forms and actual surreal values}
\label{meas:sec:hermitian}

\subsection{Positive forms without a positive strong real-point measure}

Extend $L$ to $\KC[X]$ and set $\inner PQ=L(P^*Q)$, conjugate-linear in the first
argument and linear in the second; it is Hermitian because $L$ commutes with
conjugation, and by Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:example}, $\inner PP>0$ for $P\ne0$. It is
a positive-definite algebraic Hermitian form with diagonal values in $\KR$. The
finite Cauchy--Schwarz calculation holds,
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:CS}
 \abs{\inner PQ}^2\le\inner PP\inner QQ,
\end{equation}
by nonnegativity of the squared norm of $Q-P\inner PQ/\inner PP$ when
$P\ne0$; the case $P=0$ is immediate. Equality holds exactly for linearly
dependent $P,Q$; no completion or Hilbert-space
representation is needed. Multiplication by $X$ is symmetric and satisfies
$\inner{XP}P>0$ and $\inner{(a-X)P}P>0$ for $P\ne0$; all its finite cyclic
compressions have positive spectral weights and eigenvalues in the real closed
field, as in Section~\ref{meas:sec:quadrature}. Yet its cyclic moments
$\inner1{X^n}$ have no positive strong scalar representing measure on the
ordinary real interval. So no spectral-measure proposal that would necessarily
produce such a scalar strong measure can represent these moments on that
sample domain. This does not rule out different operator topologies, different
notions of additivity, or spectra containing nonreal Hahn points. It is the
strong-class, interval counterpart of the coefficientwise unitary example on the
circle in the Herglotz report~\cite{repoherg}.

\subsection{Embedding the construction into the surreals}

With $\Gamma=\Q\subseteq\No$ and Convention~\ref{meas:conv:surreal}, the moments
become
\begin{equation}\label{meas:eq:surrealmoments}
 M_0=1,\qquad
 M_k=a^k+\sum_{n\ge1}(x_n^k-a^k)\,\omega^{-n/(n+1)}+a^k\omega^{-1}\qquad(k\ge1),
\end{equation}
all sums being normal-form strong sums. The forced negative event is
$\mu_*(\{0\})=-\omega^{-1}$, the earlier weights are $\omega^{-n/(n+1)}$, and the
anchor is $1-\sum_n\omega^{-n/(n+1)}+\omega^{-1}$. The polynomial, determinant,
quadrature and Hermitian conclusions transport along
\eqref{meas:eq:surreal-map}. The Cantor-space pattern is likewise a literal
surreal example with $t=\omega^{-1}$.

\begin{corollary}[No positive surreal strong measure for the moments;
source 11]\label{meas:cor:actualNo}
There is no positive $\No$-valued strongly countably additive measure on the
ordinary Borel interval $[0,1]$ whose polynomial moments are
\eqref{meas:eq:surrealmoments}, where ``strongly'' uses normal-form Hahn
summability. The same construction gives positive-definite Hermitian moment
forms over $\No[i]$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Suppose such a measure existed. Its domain is a set, so its range and the union
of the exponent supports of its values are sets in a class theory with
replacement. The rational span of the negatives of those exponents together
with $\Q$ is a set-sized divisible ordered subgroup $\Gamma\subseteq\No$, and
every value lies in the image of \eqref{meas:eq:surreal-map}. Pulling back gives a
positive strong Hahn measure in $\R((t^\Gamma))$ on the same ordinary sample
space, and the coefficient reconstruction of
Theorem~\ref{meas:thm:momentcriterion} in this larger group forces the mass $-t$
at $0$, a contradiction. For Hermitian positivity over all of $\No[i]$: any one
finite-degree polynomial has finitely many coefficients with set-sized
supports; enlarge $\Gamma$ to contain them. The barrier proof is valid for this
arbitrary group (the early exponents stay below $1$ and the leading ordinary
coefficient polynomial still has finite degree), so the transported form is
positive on every nonzero surcomplex polynomial.
\end{proof}

This localization uses neither a set of all surreals nor a proper-class sum.

\part{Coefficientwise Hahn measures}\label{meas:part:coef}

Sections~\ref{meas:sec:coef-extension}--\ref{meas:sec:coef-complex} of this part
are source~15's; Sections~\ref{meas:cw:sec:control}--\ref{meas:cw:sec:boundary},
on countably many exponents, are source~16's. Every measure in it is a coefficientwise Hahn measure
(Definition~\ref{meas:def:coefficientwise}) and, unless it is proved, is
\emph{not} strong. Leading coefficients may be diffuse, standard parts may have
infinitely many atoms, and two exponents suffice for hidden negative mass. Here
